# PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents

Jian Zhong ^(†)^(†)thanks: Equal contribution. Affiliation: Nankai
University Email: [zhongjian@mail.nankai.edu.cn](mailto:)    Zeyu Liu   
Pingchuan Cao Affiliation: Nankai University    Rongduo Han
Affiliation: Nankai University    Shunye Tang Affiliation: Nankai
University    Guohuan Xie Affiliation: Tsinghua University    Mingyuan
Qin Affiliation: Huazhong University of Science and Technology    Ran Ji
Affiliation: University of the Chinese Academy of Sciences    Xiao Liang
Affiliation: vivo AI Lab    Wei Wang ^(†)^(†)thanks: Corresponding
authors. Affiliation: Nankai University    Haining Zhang

###### Abstract

Long-horizon dialogue agents need to keep user memories stable enough
for personalization while adapting when the user genuinely changes.
Existing memory systems often append every observation or overwrite old
entries by heuristic rules, which makes transient noise hard to
distinguish from real belief drift. We introduce PreCoMem, a memory
consolidation framework inspired by predictive coding that represents
memory as confidence-weighted beliefs about the user’s state. Its core
signal, _Effective Surprisal_, combines semantic deviation and
contradiction evidence, then downweights this signal when retrieval is
diffuse and unreliable. According to effective surprisal, a
two-threshold gate maps each turn to one of three deterministic updates:
MAINTAIN reinforces supported beliefs, PROFILE stores ambiguous cues as
low-confidence hypotheses, and CORRECT softly decays reliably
contradicted facts. The PROFILE stage acts as an explicit waiting room,
preventing premature commitment to noisy evidence. Experiments on
LoCoMo, LongMemEval-S, and PersonaMem-v2 show consistent
state-of-the-art accuracy; our new DiMoBench confirms that PreCoMem
adapts to non-stationary belief drift without over-reacting to noise.
The code is available at
[https://github.com/ZhongWishing/PreCoMem](https://github.com/ZhongWishing/PreCoMem).

## 1 Introduction

![Refer to caption](2610.29467v1/abstract.png)

Figure 1: Memory evolution case study (coffee $`\rightarrow`$ tea).
PreCoMem autonomously manages the lifecycle of facts and hypotheses,
achieving a stable transition between conflicting belief states without
manual intervention.

Large Language Models (LLMs) are moving from stateless responders toward
long-term partners that interact with the user over hundreds or
thousands of turns \[[32](#bib.bib2), [39](#bib.bib13),
[44](#bib.bib1)\]. Such partners need more than stored transcripts; they
require a user model to sustain personalized
interaction \[[51](#bib.bib4), [15](#bib.bib34)\]. To formalize the core
units of this user model, we define a _belief_ as a compact, actionable
claim about the user’s preferences, goals, or life context, paired with
a confidence weight that can be reinforced or revised over time. This
user model underpins both agent personalization and behavioral
self-correction, thereby redefining the role of long-term memory: rather
than merely serving as simple context extension \[[30](#bib.bib3),
[41](#bib.bib5)\], long-term memory enables the agent to maintain
consistent behavior across sessions.

The harder question is not _whether_ information can be stored, but
_how_ it should be consolidated and revised. A practical memory
consolidation mechanism must decide which observations to retain, how
strongly to trust them, and when to maintain, revise, or correct stored
items without explicit supervision. We refer to this as the memory
stability–plasticity problem: the agent should preserve stable user
traits while remaining plastic enough to adapt when those traits
change \[[6](#bib.bib39)\]. Long-horizon interaction makes the problem
hard for three coupled reasons. First, most turns carry little signal,
while a small fraction may contain cues indicative of more persistent
user states. Second, user preferences and life facts evolve over
time \[[5](#bib.bib6)\], so the system must adapt under
*non-stationarity and belief drift* \[[49](#bib.bib40),
[7](#bib.bib41)\]. Third, corrective feedback is rarely
explicit \[[14](#bib.bib9)\], and noisy retrieval over long contexts
further blurs the picture \[[48](#bib.bib22), [25](#bib.bib7),
[4](#bib.bib8), [43](#bib.bib38)\]. Therefore, the brittle memory update
mechanism causes two opposite failures: premature correction from noise,
or persistent over-reliance on outdated memories, which we call
_old-memory pull_, a phenomenon related to loss of plasticity and
retrieval interference in continual adaptation
systems \[[6](#bib.bib39), [11](#bib.bib42)\].

Recent memory systems have made impressive progress on retrieval and
storage organization. Hierarchical and OS-style
stores \[[30](#bib.bib3), [23](#bib.bib35), [10](#bib.bib25)\],
structured indices with timestamps or topic shards \[[34](#bib.bib26),
[40](#bib.bib15), [21](#bib.bib32), [38](#bib.bib36)\], and agentic
retrievers \[[45](#bib.bib17), [46](#bib.bib27)\] all sharpen _what_ the
system can recall. However, the question of _when_ to update is still
often handled by recency overwriting, fixed-threshold importance, or
one-shot append rules \[[51](#bib.bib4), [2](#bib.bib16)\]. These
triggers cannot reliably separate persistent drift from transient
noise \[[11](#bib.bib42)\], so they collapse the stability–plasticity
trade-off precisely where long-horizon dialogue stresses it most. A
fuller literature discussion is provided in
Appendix [Appendix A](#A1 "Appendix A Related work ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents").

We draw inspiration from _predictive coding_, which posits that learning
is driven by mismatch between expectation and reality, i.e., prediction
error \[[8](#bib.bib10), [3](#bib.bib18)\]. In information theory the
same quantity is called *surprisal* \[[19](#bib.bib11),
[29](#bib.bib12)\]. We use this view as a design principle for the
update path rather than as a claim that dialogue memory is a fully
specified probabilistic model. The resulting criterion is simple: events
consistent with current memory should be reinforced, while surprising
events may require learning or correction \[[33](#bib.bib20),
[36](#bib.bib19)\]. Crucially, surprisal must be paired with a measure
of _retrieval reliability_, so that update decisions stay conservative
when recall itself is uncertain or noisy \[[18](#bib.bib21),
[48](#bib.bib22)\].

Building on this view, we propose PreCoMem (Predictive Cognitive
Memory), a self-evolving long-term memory framework for conversational
LLM agents. PreCoMem operates in two active phases over a shared memory
store. In _memory leveraging_, it retrieves confidence-weighted beliefs
to condition response generation. In _memory evolution_, it compares new
observations with recalled evidence and computes _Effective Surprisal_
$`S_{\mathrm{eff}}`$, a reliability-adjusted mismatch score. A
two-threshold gate then routes each update to MAINTAIN, PROFILE, or
CORRECT, corresponding to consistent, ambiguous, and reliably
contradictory evidence.
Figure [1](#S1.F1 "Figure 1 ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
illustrates this loop on a coffee-to-tea preference shift, where a
low-confidence hypothesis is gradually promoted to a fact while the
contradicted old fact is softly decayed.

In summary, we (i) propose PreCoMem, a memory consolidation framework
that casts long-term memory as a dynamic prediction of the user’s state;
(ii) introduce a _reliability-weighted surprisal signal_ that gates each
update through MAINTAIN, PROFILE, or CORRECT; and (iii) evaluate on
LoCoMo, LongMemEval-S, PersonaMem-v2, and DiMoBench/DiMoBench-XL,
controlled write-path stress tests with gradual drift and decoy
retrieval. PreCoMem outperforms the strongest contemporary baselines
while keeping effective memory stable across 1050 turns.

## 2 PreCoMem

### 2.1 Framework overview

![Refer to caption](2610.29467v1/framework.png)

Figure 2: The PreCoMem framework. Two active phases operate over a
shared memory store. Phase 1, Memory Leveraging: a confidence-modulated
retrieval score combines semantic, lexical, and topic signals to select
Top-$`K`$ beliefs $`\mathcal{C}_{t}`$ for response generation. Phase 2,
Memory Evolution: the effective surprisal $`S_{\mathrm{eff}}`$ and a
two-threshold gate route each turn to MAINTAIN, PROFILE, or CORRECT,
illustrated by representative low/medium/high-$`S_{\mathrm{eff}}`$
turns. Memory Store: an editable Belief Store $`\mathcal{B}`$ of
FACT/HYPO nodes with confidence weights, linked by provenance to an
immutable Episodic Cache $`\mathcal{E}`$ under a shared topic index;
belief items are updated independently, and the drawn links are
provenance pointers and topic groupings
(Appendix [Section C.11](#A3.SS11.SSS0.Px1 "Memory store serialization and example snapshot. ‣ C.11 System inspectability, traces, and qualitative analyses ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")).

As illustrated in
[Figure 2](#S2.F2 "In 2.1 Framework overview ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents"),
PreCoMem operates as a closed loop of two active phases around a shared
memory store. Memory Leveraging retrieves confidence-weighted beliefs
and uses them to condition response generation. Memory Evolution then
revises the memory state in light of the new interaction: it computes
_Effective Surprisal_ $`S_{\mathrm{eff}}`$ against recalled evidence,
and routes the update to one of three deterministic operators—MAINTAIN,
PROFILE, or CORRECT. This read–write loop differs from prior memory
systems in two ways. First, every belief item carries a continuous
confidence weight $`w_{t}(m)\!\in\![0,1]`$ that is reinforced or softly
decayed by the operators, instead of relying on discrete commit
decisions such as Add/Ignore/Update in O-Mem \[[40](#bib.bib15)\] or
binary expiry intervals in EverMemOS \[[10](#bib.bib25)\]. Second,
PROFILE buffers ambiguous observations as low-confidence hypotheses,
letting weak evidence accumulate across turns before it is committed or
discarded.

In implementation, each operator pairs a closed-form update rule with a
fixed-prompt LLM sub-agent. The sub-agent only identifies which items
are relevant and emits one bounded scalar, such as evidence_strength,
severity, or initial_confidence. The deterministic layer then plugs
these scalars into
[Equations 7](#S2.E7 "In MAINTAIN (reinforcement). ‣ 2.4 Memory evolution: regime-gated belief updating ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents"),
[8](#S2.E8 "Equation 8 ‣ PROFILE (hypothesis writing). ‣ 2.4 Memory evolution: regime-gated belief updating ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
and [10](#S2.E10 "Equation 10 ‣ CORRECT (soft correction). ‣ 2.4 Memory evolution: regime-gated belief updating ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents").
Thus the LLM proposes local magnitudes, while the gate, confidence
bounds, and closed-form $`w`$ updates control when and how memory
changes accumulate.
Algorithm [1](#alg1 "Algorithm 1 ‣ Cold start. ‣ B.2 Retrieval scoring and surprisal component definitions ‣ Appendix B Additional method details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
(Appendix [Section B.4](#A2.SS4 "B.4 Per-turn pipeline (hybrid execution) ‣ Appendix B Additional method details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents"))
gives the per-turn pipeline.

### 2.2 Memory primitives

Dialogue and memory state. At turn $`t=1,2,\ldots`$, the user input is
$`u_{t}`$, the agent response is $`y_{t}`$, and $`Q_{t-1}`$ is a rolling
working-memory window. Long-term memory is
$`\mathcal{M}_{t}=(\mathcal{B}_{t},\mathcal{E}_{t},\mathcal{T}_{t})`$.
The belief store $`\mathcal{B}_{t}`$ contains editable facts and
hypotheses with confidence weights. The episodic cache
$`\mathcal{E}_{t}`$ stores immutable raw dialogue traces with source
pointers. The optional topic table $`\mathcal{T}_{t}`$ is only a
retrieval prior. This keeps beliefs editable and evidence traceable.

Items, confidence, and hypothesis lifecycle. A memory item $`m`$ has
content $`c(m)`$, type
$`\tau(m)\in\{\texttt{FACT},\texttt{HYPOTHESIS}\}`$, and retrieval
metadata such as embeddings, topic labels, entities, timestamps, and
source pointers into $`\mathcal{E}_{t}`$. For belief items,
$`w_{t}(m)\in[0,1]`$ is an operational support score. Episodic traces
are fixed at $`w_{t}(e)\equiv 1`$ and used only as provenance, not in
confidence-weighted Top-$`K`$ ranking. A HYPOTHESIS is a provisional
belief: it is reinforced under supporting evidence, decayed when
evidence stays absent, and promoted to FACT when confidence crosses
$`\tau_{\mathrm{promote}}`$. Type transitions are one-way. Contradicted
facts are softly decayed by CORRECT
([Section 2.4](#S2.SS4 "2.4 Memory evolution: regime-gated belief updating ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents"))
rather than demoted, yielding low-confidence “deprecated” facts that
confidence-weighted retrieval naturally deprioritizes.
Appendix [Section C.7](#A3.SS7.SSS0.Px3 "FACT pruning ablation. ‣ C.7 Memory dynamics: footprint, contamination, and retention ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
confirms this retention is accuracy-neutral.

### 2.3 Memory leveraging: evidence recall and response generation

In Memory Leveraging, PreCoMem recalls a compact evidence set from
long-term memory and leverages it to generate the current response.

Evidence recall. Given $`u_{t}`$ and short-term context $`Q_{t-1}`$,
PreCoMem ranks candidates in $`\mathcal{B}_{t}`$ with a
confidence-modulated score. The score combines three retrieval signals:
semantic similarity, lexical overlap (BM25), and topic relevance.
Omitting $`Q_{t-1}`$ in the notation,

|     |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|     |

````math
s(u_{t},m)=w_{t}(m)\sum_{j=1}^{3}\alpha_{j}s_{j}(u_{t},m),\quad m\in\mathcal{B}_{t},
``` |  | (1) |

where
$`(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})=(s_{\mathrm{sem}},s_{\mathrm{fact}},s_{\mathrm{topic}})`$
and $`\boldsymbol{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})^{\top}`$ is
a convex weight vector. The multiplier $`w_{t}(m)`$ biases retrieval
toward high-confidence items. The Top-$`K`$ belief items form
$`\mathcal{C}_{t}=\{m_{i}\}_{i=1}^{K}`$ with retrieval scores
$`\mathbf{s}_{t}`$. Linked episodic traces from $`\mathcal{E}_{t}`$ are
appended as provenance context, but they do not participate in Top-$`K`$
ranking. The same scores $`\mathbf{s}_{t}`$ are retained for the
reliability estimate in
[Section 2.4](#S2.SS4 "2.4 Memory evolution: regime-gated belief updating ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents").

Evidence-conditioned response. The recalled evidence texts
$`\{c(m)\mid m\in\mathcal{C}_{t}\}`$, together with $`u_{t}`$ and the
short-term window $`Q_{t-1}`$, condition the response model:

|     |                                                        |     |     |
|-----|--------------------------------------------------------|-----|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       y_{t}=\texttt{Respond}(u_{t},Q_{t-1},\mathcal{C}_{t}).
       ```                                                     |     | (2) |

We reuse the same recall outputs $`(\mathcal{C}_{t},\mathbf{s}_{t})`$ to
perform memory evolution in
[Section 2.4](#S2.SS4 "2.4 Memory evolution: regime-gated belief updating ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents").

### 2.4 Memory evolution: regime-gated belief updating

Given the recall outputs $`(\mathcal{C}_{t},\mathbf{s}_{t})`$ and the
new interaction $`(u_{t},y_{t})`$, PreCoMem computes a prediction-error
signal adjusted by retrieval reliability, and applies a regime-gated
update to the belief store. It also writes the new turn back into the
episodic cache for future provenance and recall.

Retrieval reliability. Surprisal computed against recalled evidence can
be misleading when recall is uncertain, so we derive a reliability
coefficient from the entropy of retrieval scores. Let
$`\mathbf{s}_{t}=\{s_{i}\}_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{C}_{t}|}`$ denote the
Top-$`K`$ retrieval scores returned with $`\mathcal{C}_{t}`$, and let
$`\hat{s}_{i}=(s_{i}+\epsilon)/\sum_{j}(s_{j}+\epsilon)`$ be the
smoothed normalization. We define the normalized entropy $`H_{t}`$ and
the reliability coefficient $`\rho_{t}`$ as

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
H_{t}=\frac{-\sum_{i}\hat{s}_{i}\log\hat{s}_{i}}{\log\max\{|\mathcal{C}_{t}|,2\}}\in[0,1],\qquad\rho_{t}=1-H_{t}.
``` |  | (3) |

Because $`H_{t}`$ is minimized when one item dominates and maximized
under near-uniform scores, $`\rho_{t}`$ measures score concentration as
a proxy for evidence precision. Diffuse recall (small $`\rho_{t}`$)
downweights deviation signals to avoid over-updating; concentrated
recall (large $`\rho_{t}`$) preserves them for correction. Thus the gate
becomes aggressive only when recall is concentrated and the raw
contradiction/deviation signal is large. Since $`\rho_{t}`$ enters only
multiplicatively, it can defer an update but never trigger one on its
own; whether CORRECT fires still depends on the contradiction term
below, and
Appendix [Section C.8](#A3.SS8.SSS0.Px2 "What 𝜌_𝑡 measures: calibration. ‣ C.8 Retrieval dependence and reliability calibration ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
verifies empirically that $`\rho_{t}`$ tracks evidence precision. The
constant $`\epsilon>0`$ ensures numerical stability.

Raw surprisal proxy. We define:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
S_{\mathrm{raw}}(u_{t})=\lambda\,d_{\mathrm{sem}}(u_{t},\mathcal{C}_{t})+(1-\lambda)\,d_{\mathrm{conf}}(u_{t},\mathcal{C}_{t}),
``` |  | (4) |

where $`\lambda\in(0,1)`$, $`d_{\mathrm{sem}}`$ is the normalized
semantic deviation between $`u_{t}`$ and score-weighted recalled
evidence, and $`d_{\mathrm{conf}}`$ is a verifier-based contradiction
score quantifying how strongly $`u_{t}`$ conflicts with recalled beliefs
(see
Appendix [Section B.2](#A2.SS2.SSS0.Px2 "Surprisal components. ‣ B.2 Retrieval scoring and surprisal component definitions ‣ Appendix B Additional method details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")).

Effective surprisal. We define:

|     |                                                            |     |     |
|-----|------------------------------------------------------------|-----|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       S_{\mathrm{eff}}(u_{t})=\rho_{t}\,S_{\mathrm{raw}}(u_{t}),
       ```                                                         |     | (5) |

which corresponds to a precision-weighted prediction error that becomes
conservative when recall is unreliable. We use
$`x_{t}:=S_{\mathrm{eff}}(u_{t})`$ as the scalar gate score below.
Appendix [Section B.1](#A2.SS1 "B.1 From expected predictive loss to a per-turn on-policy loss ‣ Appendix B Additional method details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
gives an optimization view that interprets $`x_{t}`$ as a Monte Carlo
surrogate of a long-horizon predictive objective.

Regime-gated evolution. Given two ordered thresholds
$`\theta_{\mathrm{low}}<\theta_{\mathrm{high}}`$, PreCoMem maps the gate
score $`x_{t}`$ into three regimes:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
a_{t}=\begin{cases}\texttt{MAINTAIN},&x_{t}\leq\theta_{\mathrm{low}}\\
\texttt{PROFILE},&\theta_{\mathrm{low}}<x_{t}\leq\theta_{\mathrm{high}}\\
\texttt{CORRECT},&x_{t}>\theta_{\mathrm{high}}.\end{cases}
``` |  | (6) |

Two-threshold gate. Low, medium, and high $`x_{t}`$ correspond to
consistent, ambiguous, and reliably contradictory evidence. PROFILE
handles the middle regime that a binary maintain/correct rule must
either ignore or overwrite. We fix
$`(\theta_{\mathrm{low}},\theta_{\mathrm{high}})=(0.2,0.6)`$ throughout
the main experiments to keep behavior controllable and the comparison
reproducible across benchmarks; the cost-sensitive break-even
derivation, operator ablations, threshold sensitivity, and
dynamic-threshold variant are in
Appendix [Sections B.3](#A2.SS3.SSS0.Px3 "Two-threshold gate. ‣ B.3 Operator and gate derivations ‣ Appendix B Additional method details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents"),
[C.4](#A3.SS4.SSS0.Px3 "DiMoBench evolution-operator ablations. ‣ C.4 Component, operator, and control ablations ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents"),
[C.5](#A3.SS5.SSS0.Px6 "Sensitivity to gating thresholds. ‣ C.5 Hyperparameter sensitivity and adaptive variants ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
and [C.5](#A3.SS5.SSS0.Px7 "Adaptive thresholding for personalization. ‣ C.5 Hyperparameter sensitivity and adaptive variants ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents").

#### MAINTAIN (reinforcement).

When surprisal is low, PreCoMem strengthens supported beliefs in
$`\mathcal{C}_{t}`$ via:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
w_{t+1}(m)=w_{t}(m)+\eta_{t}(m)\,(1-w_{t}(m)),\quad m\in\mathcal{C}_{t}\cap\mathcal{B}_{t},
``` |  | (7) |

where $`\eta_{t}(m)\in(0,1]`$ is the per-item evidence_strength returned
by the Maintain-Agent; weakly supported items barely move, while
strongly supported ones are pulled toward $`1`$. The optimization view
in
Appendix [Section B.3](#A2.SS3 "B.3 Operator and gate derivations ‣ Appendix B Additional method details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
treats $`\eta_{t}(m)`$ as a scalar step size.

#### PROFILE (hypothesis writing).

When surprisal is medium, the observation is novel but not decisively
contradictory. PreCoMem generates a low-confidence hypothesis $`h`$ and
writes it as a HYPOTHESIS belief:

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle h`$ | $`\displaystyle=\texttt{GenerateHypothesis}(u_{t},\mathcal{C}_{t}),\qquad\tau(h)=\texttt{HYPOTHESIS},`$ |  | (8) |
|  | $`\displaystyle b_{t}^{(c)}`$ | $`\displaystyle=w_{\min}^{(c)}+\sigma(x_{t})\bigl(w_{\max}^{(c)}-w_{\min}^{(c)}\bigr),\qquad w_{0}(h)=\tfrac{1}{2}\,b_{t}^{(c)}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\mathrm{clip}\bigl(q_{t},[w_{\min}^{(c)},w_{\max}^{(c)}]\bigr),`$ |  |  |

where GenerateHypothesis is the Profile-Agent under a fixed prompt,
$`\sigma(x)=1/(1+e^{-x})`$,
$`c\!\in\!\{\textsc{explicit},\textsc{latent}\}`$ is the class label
assigned by the agent, and $`q_{t}`$ is the agent’s initial_confidence,
clipped to the class band $`[w_{\min}^{(c)},w_{\max}^{(c)}]`$. The gate
score thus supplies a reproducible prior $`b_{t}^{(c)}`$ inside the
band, and the agent scalar can shift $`w_{0}`$ by at most half the band
width. *Explicit-event* hypotheses paraphrase stated facts or actions
and use the band $`[0.60,0.70]`$. *Latent-inference* hypotheses capture
deduced traits, moods, or hidden goals and use $`[0.50,0.59]`$. These
initial values are low relative to stable FACTs, which sit at
$`w\!\geq\!0.80`$ and meet the promotion threshold
$`\tau_{\mathrm{promote}}\!=\!0.80`$. A fresh hypothesis therefore ranks
below committed facts in confidence-weighted retrieval
([Equation 1](#S2.E1 "In 2.3 Memory leveraging: evidence recall and response generation ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents"))
and still needs repeated support to graduate. In later turns, hypotheses
are reinforced under MAINTAIN when supported and decayed when evidence
remains absent:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
w_{t}(h)\leftarrow w_{t}(h)\exp\bigl(-\gamma\,a_{t}(h)\bigr),\qquad a_{t}(h)=t-\ell(h),
``` |  | (9) |

where $`\ell(h)`$ is the turn at which $`h`$ was created or last
received supporting evidence and $`\gamma>0`$ is a decay rate. The rule
is applied once per turn to unsupported hypotheses: any supporting
update resets $`\ell(h)`$ and skips the decay in that turn, and
hypotheses flagged as decay_protection by the agent (explicit events)
are exempt, so the compounding decay over consecutive unsupported turns
(cumulative factor $`\exp(-\gamma\,n(n+1)/2)`$ after $`n`$ turns;
Appendix [Section B.3](#A2.SS3.SSS0.Px5 "Hypothesis decay horizon. ‣ B.3 Operator and gate derivations ‣ Appendix B Additional method details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents"))
prunes only guesses that are never revalidated. Hypotheses are promoted
to facts once confidence crosses $`\tau_{\mathrm{promote}}`$, with
$`0\!\leq\!w_{\min}^{(c)}\!<\!w_{\max}^{(c)}\!<\!\tau_{\mathrm{promote}}\!\leq\!1`$
enforced for every class so that promotion always requires repeated
support.

#### CORRECT (soft correction).

When surprisal is high and recall is reliable, the new observation
likely contradicts existing high-confidence beliefs. PreCoMem records
the new observation as a fresh belief node (a FACT with initial weight
$`0.85`$) and separately decays the retrieved beliefs it contradicts.
The decay side is:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
w_{t+1}(m)=w_{t}(m)\exp\!\bigl(-\beta\,x_{t}\,\zeta_{t}(m)\bigr),\quad m\in\mathcal{C}_{t}\cap\mathcal{B}_{t},
``` |  | (10) |

where $`\beta>0`$ controls the correction strength and
$`\zeta_{t}(m)\!\in\![0,1]`$ is the per-item *contradiction severity*
returned by the Correct-Agent; non-contradictory items receive
$`\zeta_{t}(m)\!\approx\!0`$ and are essentially unchanged, enabling
selective correction without extra thresholds. We deliberately separate
this context-aware decay magnitude from the fixed NLI contradiction
score that feeds $`d_{\mathrm{conf}}`$
([Equation 22](#A2.E22 "In Surprisal components. ‣ B.2 Retrieval scoring and surprisal component definitions ‣ Appendix B Additional method details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
in
Appendix [Section B.2](#A2.SS2.SSS0.Px2 "Surprisal components. ‣ B.2 Retrieval scoring and surprisal component definitions ‣ Appendix B Additional method details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")):
the NLI score drives the gate via $`S_{\mathrm{raw}}`$, whereas
$`\zeta_{t}`$ localizes how strongly each retrieved belief should decay.
A derivation is provided in
Appendix [Section B.3](#A2.SS3.SSS0.Px2 "CORRECT update. ‣ B.3 Operator and gate derivations ‣ Appendix B Additional method details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents").

#### Trace and topic write-back.

After generating $`y_{t}`$, PreCoMem appends the turn trace
$`(u_{t},y_{t})`$ to the episodic cache $`\mathcal{E}_{t+1}`$ with a
fixed-capacity eviction policy; optionally, new topics are added to
$`\mathcal{T}_{t+1}`$ when detected, yielding
$`\mathcal{M}_{t+1}=(\mathcal{B}_{t+1},\mathcal{E}_{t+1},\mathcal{T}_{t+1})`$.
When PROFILE/MAINTAIN/CORRECT creates or updates belief items, we record
source pointers to supporting traces so beliefs remain editable while
evidence remains traceable.

## 3 Experiments

### 3.1 Experimental setup

Benchmarks. We evaluate on LoCoMo \[[28](#bib.bib33)\] (1,500+ questions
in long contexts), LongMemEval-S \[[43](#bib.bib38)\] (500 questions
covering six question types over five long-term memory abilities), and
PersonaMem-v2 \[[16](#bib.bib37)\] ($`1{,}000`$ personas and
$`20{,}000+`$ implicit preferences, evaluated under both 32k and 128k
contexts;
[Figure 3](#S2.F3 "In Trace and topic write-back. ‣ 2.4 Memory evolution: regime-gated belief updating ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")(c)–(f)).
To stress-test belief drift under sparse signals we additionally
introduce DiMoBench, which adds controlled non-stationarity and decoy
retrieval
([Section 3.3](#S3.SS3 "3.3 Ablation studies and belief-drift adaptation ‣ 3 Experiments ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")).
Two third-party benchmarks, AMemGym \[[1](#bib.bib48)\] and
LoCoMo-Plus \[[22](#bib.bib49)\], are evaluated under their official
protocols in
Appendix [Section C.1](#A3.SS1 "C.1 Third-party benchmarks: AMemGym and LoCoMo-Plus ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents").

Baselines. We compare PreCoMem against O-Mem \[[40](#bib.bib15)\],
MemoryOS \[[17](#bib.bib14)\], MemOS \[[23](#bib.bib35)\],
Mem0 \[[2](#bib.bib16)\], A-Mem \[[45](#bib.bib17)\],
GAM \[[46](#bib.bib27)\], MemBox \[[38](#bib.bib36)\],
TiMem \[[21](#bib.bib32)\], and EverMemOS \[[10](#bib.bib25)\], each
under its officially recommended configuration; we standardize the
backbone LLM and embedding model across methods whenever applicable.

Evaluation protocol. We adopt the LLM-as-a-judge protocol from
MemOS \[[23](#bib.bib35)\]: each answer is scored by a primary judge
(gpt-4.1-mini) and two auxiliary gemini-2.5-flash judges (run with
distinct system prompts to reduce judge-correlation) in a blinded setup,
with accuracy reported as the average of the three binary
judgments \[[20](#bib.bib44), [9](#bib.bib43), [12](#bib.bib45),
[35](#bib.bib46), [47](#bib.bib47)\].
Appendix [Section C.3](#A3.SS3.SSS0.Px2 "LLM-as-a-judge validation across benchmarks. ‣ C.3 Extended results and judge validation ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
reports human–LLM agreement on LoCoMo, LongMemEval-S, and DiMoBench plus
a cross-method spot-check on baseline outputs.

Implementation details. PreCoMem uses gpt-4.1 (or gpt-4o-mini) for all
LLM-based reasoning and memory operations and Qwen3-Embedding-0.6B for
dense embeddings across methods. Unless otherwise specified, $`k{=}10`$,
$`(\theta_{\mathrm{low}},\theta_{\mathrm{high}})=(0.2,0.6)`$,
$`\lambda{=}0.5`$, $`\beta{=}1.0`$,
$`\boldsymbol{\alpha}{=}(0.4,0.4,0.2)`$, and $`\gamma{=}0.05`$;
sensitivity studies and the adaptive variant are in
Appendix [Sections C.5](#A3.SS5.SSS0.Px1 "Sensitivity to retrieval budget 𝑘. ‣ C.5 Hyperparameter sensitivity and adaptive variants ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents"),
[C.5](#A3.SS5.SSS0.Px6 "Sensitivity to gating thresholds. ‣ C.5 Hyperparameter sensitivity and adaptive variants ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
and [C.5](#A3.SS5.SSS0.Px7 "Adaptive thresholding for personalization. ‣ C.5 Hyperparameter sensitivity and adaptive variants ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents").

### 3.2 Main results

Main results are reported in
[Table 1](#S2.T1 "In Trace and topic write-back. ‣ 2.4 Memory evolution: regime-gated belief updating ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents"):
the upper block on LoCoMo and the lower block on LongMemEval-S. We make
three observations.

\(1\) Overall performance. PreCoMem achieves the best overall accuracy
on both tabulated benchmarks. On LoCoMo it leads the strongest baseline
(TiMem) by $`+1.69`$ with gpt-4o-mini ($`73.57`$ vs. $`71.88`$) and
$`+1.02`$ with gpt-4.1 ($`85.06`$ vs. $`84.04`$); both gaps exceed the
cross-seed standard deviation of either system reported in
[Table 1](#S2.T1 "In Trace and topic write-back. ‣ 2.4 Memory evolution: regime-gated belief updating ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents").
On LongMemEval-S it reaches $`89.39`$ under gpt-4.1 ($`+3.23`$ over
EverMemOS), and the gpt-4o-mini appendix block follows the same pattern
($`84.28`$, $`+1.96`$;
Appendix [Section C.3](#A3.SS3.SSS0.Px1 "LongMemEval-S results under gpt-4o-mini. ‣ C.3 Extended results and judge validation ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")).
On PersonaMem-v2
([Figure 3](#S2.F3 "In Trace and topic write-back. ‣ 2.4 Memory evolution: regime-gated belief updating ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")(e)–(f))
under gpt-4.1, PreCoMem reaches $`72.31`$ Open-Ended at 32k and
$`67.84`$ at 128k, $`+3.37`$ and $`+2.89`$ over EverMemOS. The advantage
also holds on two third-party benchmarks we did not design: $`0.458`$
Overall / $`0.329`$ normalized Memory on AMemGym against $`0.430`$ /
$`0.296`$ for the strongest system reported there, and $`22.4`$ on
LoCoMo-Plus against $`21.05`$ for full-context GPT-4o
(Appendix [Section C.1](#A3.SS1 "C.1 Third-party benchmarks: AMemGym and LoCoMo-Plus ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")).
[Figure 3](#S2.F3 "In Trace and topic write-back. ‣ 2.4 Memory evolution: regime-gated belief updating ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")(a)–(d)
further shows that, with the answer model held fixed, delegating memory
construction to PreCoMem on a one-tier-smaller backbone still beats the
larger backbone’s native memory in all twelve comparisons.

\(2\) Where gains come from. On LoCoMo, the gain concentrates on the
Open-Domain split (PreCoMem $`48.23`$ under gpt-4.1, $`+1.30`$ over
MemOS; consistently best under gpt-4o-mini), whose questions typically
ask for a latent state that is not stated in a single turn and must be
inferred from cues spread across sessions
(Appendix [Section C.3](#A3.SS3.SSS0.Px4 "Category-level annotation supporting the attribution. ‣ C.3 Extended results and judge validation ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")),
while EverMemOS, O-Mem, and TiMem retain narrow leads on Single-Hop /
Multi-Hop / Temporal under gpt-4.1 from their dedicated read-path
designs. On LongMemEval-S (gpt-4.1), the largest gains occur on the
question types whose answers depend on updating or aggregating
user-specific memory: SSP (single-session preference, $`+9.24`$ over
O-Mem), KU (knowledge update, $`+4.75`$ over O-Mem; every KU question
carries an old value superseded by a newer one), and MS (multi-session
aggregation, $`+3.55`$). The SSA gain (assistant-side single-session
information, $`+3.96`$) reflects selective memory use: the single target
session lies among $`48.9`$ sessions on average, and confidence-weighted
retrieval acts as an implicit reranker. PersonaMem-v2 reproduces this
pattern: PreCoMem’s lead is largest on anti-stereotypical, dynamic, and
ownership-disambiguation categories that most stress write-path quality,
and shrinks to under two points on stereotypical or static cues where
population priors suffice. PreCoMem’s advantage is therefore strongest
when answers require selective memory use, belief updating, or
cross-session aggregation; a structural analysis attributes the gains to
two design properties absent from contemporary baselines—continuous
per-belief confidence that buffers ambiguous cues, and a
confidence-weighted retrieval score that acts as an implicit reranker
(Appendix [Section C.10](#A3.SS10 "C.10 Structural comparison: why PreCoMem outperforms existing memory systems ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")).

\(3\) Accuracy–efficiency trade-off.
[Figure 5](#S3.F5 "In 3.5 Memory scalability ‣ 3 Experiments ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")(a)
shows the LoCoMo performance–cost frontier as we vary $`k`$: PreCoMem
peaks at $`k\!=\!10`$ with moderate token usage, while larger $`k`$
yields diminishing returns. Supporting diagnostics on hypothesis
lifecycle, Top-$`k`$ sensitivity, and memory footprint are reported in
Appendix [Sections C.3](#A3.SS3.SSS0.Px3 "Hypothesis lifecycle statistics. ‣ C.3 Extended results and judge validation ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents"),
[C.5](#A3.SS5.SSS0.Px1 "Sensitivity to retrieval budget 𝑘. ‣ C.5 Hyperparameter sensitivity and adaptive variants ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
and [C.7](#A3.SS7.SSS0.Px4 "Cross-system memory footprint. ‣ C.7 Memory dynamics: footprint, contamination, and retention ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents").

### 3.3 Ablation studies and belief-drift adaptation

Figure 4: Component ablation and DiMoBench dynamics. (a) Component
ablation on LoCoMo and LongMemEval-S. (b) Moving-average accuracy under
implicit drift on DiMoBench. (c) Operator-level ablation on DiMoBench.

Component ablation. We ablate three core components while keeping the
backbone, prompts, and hyperparameters fixed. ES removes the
effective-surprisal gate by setting the gate score to $`0`$; RR removes
retrieval-reliability adjustment via $`\rho_{t}\!\equiv\!1`$; CW removes
confidence weighting globally, in retrieval and in every lifecycle test,
by setting all belief weights to $`1`$.
[Figure 4](#S3.F4 "In 3.3 Ablation studies and belief-drift adaptation ‣ 3 Experiments ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")(a)
reports LLM-judge accuracy on LoCoMo and LongMemEval-S. Removing ES
causes the largest drop on LoCoMo ($`73.57\!\to\!31.46`$; similar on
LongMemEval-S): with the gate score zeroed every turn is routed to
MAINTAIN, so this reinforce-only variant marks the lower bound of
*surprisal-gated evolution*, the primary mechanism under
non-stationarity, while route-matched controls that isolate the gate’s
marginal value are reported in
Appendix [Section C.4](#A3.SS4.SSS0.Px5 "Route-matched and factorial controls. ‣ C.4 Component, operator, and control ablations ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents").
Removing CW ($`\!\to\!43.42`$) and RR ($`\!\to\!56.81`$) also
substantially reduces accuracy, with the same trend on LongMemEval-S.
Letting the same LLM emit the entire write decision end-to-end instead
of bounded scalars lowers accuracy by $`2.75`$ / $`3.15`$ points on
LoCoMo / LongMemEval-S and raises the cross-seed standard deviation
$`20\times`$ on LoCoMo (under gpt-4.1;
Appendix [Section C.4](#A3.SS4.SSS0.Px6 "LLM-direct-write baseline. ‣ C.4 Component, operator, and control ablations ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")).
Appendix [Section C.4](#A3.SS4.SSS0.Px1 "Fine-grained ablation of raw-surprisal components. ‣ C.4 Component, operator, and control ablations ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
shows the NLI contradiction term drives most gains.

DiMoBench setup. DiMoBench (Dialogue Implicit Memory Drift Benchmark) is
a controlled diagnostic setting for gradual attitude and preference
drift. A user’s latent stance reverses around a change point, while
keyword-matched chatter and entity confounders pollute the stream so
naive retrieval and overwrite-style updates become brittle. The released
suite has *five independent* $`350`$-turn dialogues, each driven by a
distinct persona, with $`200`$ QA pairs per dialogue ($`1{,}000`$ total;
results averaged across personas); DiMoBench-XL extends this to $`1050`$
turns and three sequential drifts, with 420 QA pairs each, 2,100 in
total
(Appendix [Sections C.6](#A3.SS6.SSS0.Px1 "Construction pipeline and characteristics. ‣ C.6 DiMoBench: construction and extended-horizon stress test ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
and [C.6](#A3.SS6.SSS0.Px5 "DiMoBench-XL construction. ‣ C.6 DiMoBench: construction and extended-horizon stress test ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")).

Update
dynamics. [Figure 4](#S3.F4 "In 3.3 Ablation studies and belief-drift adaptation ‣ 3 Experiments ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")(b)
plots LLM-judge accuracy across dialogue turns. Before the change point
all methods are stable; afterwards PreCoMem experiences a transient drop
but recovers fastest and stabilizes at $`84\%`$, while EverMemOS, TiMem,
and O-Mem plateau at 78–80% and Mem0 fails to recover ($`54\%`$).
End-to-end traces are provided in
Appendix [Section C.11](#A3.SS11.SSS0.Px4 "End-to-end trace examples on DiMoBench. ‣ C.11 System inspectability, traces, and qualitative analyses ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents").

Operator ablation on DiMoBench. Ablating each operator individually
([Figure 4](#S3.F4 "In 3.3 Ablation studies and belief-drift adaptation ‣ 3 Experiments ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")(c))
yields qualitatively distinct failure modes: removing PROFILE caps the
asymptote at $`72\%`$ (vs. $`84\%`$); removing CORRECT delays recovery
(turn $`320`$ vs. $`250`$) and lowers the plateau to $`77\%`$; removing
MAINTAIN fails to stabilize within the horizon ($`54\%`$ at the end),
confirming that the three operators play distinct stabilizing roles.
Appendix [Section C.4](#A3.SS4.SSS0.Px3 "DiMoBench evolution-operator ablations. ‣ C.4 Component, operator, and control ablations ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
provides the full analysis, and the same three qualitative signatures
reproduce on the third-party AMemGym benchmark
(Appendix [Section C.1](#A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1 "AMemGym. ‣ C.1 Third-party benchmarks: AMemGym and LoCoMo-Plus ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")).

### 3.4 Hyperparameter analysis

We sweep the retrieval budget $`k\!\in\!\{1,\ldots,20\}`$ on LoCoMo
(gpt-4.1). Accuracy rises sharply through $`k\!\approx\!6`$, peaks at
$`k\!=\!10`$ (Overall $`85.06\%`$), then drops mildly. Larger $`k`$
flattens the Top-$`k`$ score distribution, lowering $`\rho_{t}`$ and
dampening the effective surprisal $`x_{t}`$ that drives CORRECT. It also
increases the chance that one retrieved item spuriously appears
contradictory under the max-based contradiction component
$`d_{\mathrm{conf}}`$. We therefore fix $`k\!=\!10`$; the full sweep and
retrieval-latency profile are reported in
Appendix [Sections C.5](#A3.SS5.SSS0.Px1 "Sensitivity to retrieval budget 𝑘. ‣ C.5 Hyperparameter sensitivity and adaptive variants ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
and [C.9](#A3.SS9 "C.9 Retrieval and end-to-end latency analysis ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents").
Because the write path acts only on retrieved beliefs,
Appendix [Section C.8](#A3.SS8 "C.8 Retrieval dependence and reliability calibration ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
further measures how often the belief due for an update actually enters
the Top-$`k`$ and what a miss costs.

### 3.5 Memory scalability

Figure 5: Hyperparameter and memory-scalability analysis. (a) LoCoMo
accuracy–efficiency frontier as the retrieval budget $`k`$ varies;
overall accuracy peaks at $`k\!=\!10`$, beyond which additional context
yields diminishing returns and dilutes retrieval reliability
$`\rho_{t}`$. (b, c) Memory scalability under long-horizon belief drift:
raw memory grows linearly, while effective memory quickly plateaus on
both DiMoBench and DiMoBench-XL (panel (c) shows one representative
persona).

A natural concern for any long-term memory system is whether storage and
retrieval cost scale gracefully with the conversation length. We examine
this on three settings spanning natural and stress-test horizons: the
LoCoMo benchmark (ten multi-session dialogues, mean $`\bar{N}\!=\!588`$
turns), DiMoBench (350 turns, 1 drift), and DiMoBench-XL (1050 turns, 3
drifts; five personas, of which a representative one is plotted, the
others staying within $`\pm 1.4`$ of it;
Appendix [Section C.6](#A3.SS6.SSS0.Px5 "DiMoBench-XL construction. ‣ C.6 DiMoBench: construction and extended-horizon stress test ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")).
Two quantities are tracked every 10 turns on DiMoBench and every 50
turns on DiMoBench-XL:

- •
  *Raw count* $`N_{\mathrm{raw}}(t)=|\mathcal{B}_{t}|`$: the number of
  belief items physically stored, including *deprecated facts*.
  Deprecated facts are previously committed FACTs whose confidence has
  been softly decayed below $`0.1`$ by repeated CORRECT updates
  ([Equation 10](#S2.E10 "In CORRECT (soft correction). ‣ 2.4 Memory evolution: regime-gated belief updating ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents"));
  we keep them in $`\mathcal{B}_{t}`$ as provenance hooks rather than
  deleting them.
- •
  *Effective count*
  $`N_{\mathrm{eff}}(t)=\sum_{m\in\mathcal{B}_{t}}w_{t}(m)`$: the
  confidence-weighted total mass. Intuitively, this is the equivalent
  number of fully-supported items the agent “acts on” through the
  confidence-weighted retrieval score in
  [Equation 1](#S2.E1 "In 2.3 Memory leveraging: evidence recall and response generation ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents").
  A deprecated fact at $`w\!=\!0.05`$ contributes only $`0.05`$ to
  $`N_{\mathrm{eff}}`$, which matches its negligible influence on
  Top-$`K`$ ranking.

[Figure 5](#S3.F5 "In 3.5 Memory scalability ‣ 3 Experiments ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")(b,
c) shows $`N_{\mathrm{raw}}`$ grows roughly linearly with the dialogue,
but $`N_{\mathrm{eff}}`$ saturates by turn 200 (at $`25.8`$–$`28.5`$ on
the representative persona, with the other personas within $`\pm 1.4`$
of it) and, apart from a transient dip at each drift, stays in that
band. Moreover, deprecated facts occupy only $`1.0\%`$ of Top-10 slots
on average even after 1050 turns and three drifts
(Appendix [Table 19](#A3.T19 "In Headline metrics and retrieval contamination. ‣ C.7 Memory dynamics: footprint, contamination, and retention ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")).
Confidence weighting therefore turns linearly growing storage into a
roughly constant retrieval surface.

| Method | Per-item conf. | $`N_{\mathrm{raw}}^{\mathrm{end}}`$ | $`N_{\mathrm{eff}}^{\mathrm{end}}`$ | Top-10 avg. $`w`$ | LoCoMo Overall |
|----|----|----|----|----|----|
| O-Mem \[[40](#bib.bib15)\] | ✗ (Add/Ignore/Update) | 232 | – | – | 70.88 |
| EverMemOS \[[10](#bib.bib25)\] | ✗ ($`[t_{s},t_{e}]`$ expiry) | 264 | – | – | 70.13 |
| PreCoMem (A0, default) | ✓ ($`w_{t}\!\in\![0,1]`$) | 287 | 95.7 | 0.81 | 73.57 |
| PreCoMem (A1, $`w<0.05`$ prune) | ✓ ($`w_{t}\!\in\![0,1]`$) | 168 | 95.6 | 0.83 | 73.51 |

Table 2: Memory footprint vs. accuracy on the ten LoCoMo dialogues (mean
turns $`\bar{N}\!=\!588`$). All numbers are measured under the same
backbone (gpt-4o-mini) and embedding model. “Per-item conf.” indicates
whether the system maintains a continuous confidence weight per stored
item; under discrete commit decisions, the effective count
$`N_{\mathrm{eff}}`$ and the average Top-10 weight are not defined.
PreCoMem (A1) is the optional pruning configuration that drops items
with $`w_{t}\!<\!0.05`$.

[Table 2](#S3.T2 "In 3.5 Memory scalability ‣ 3 Experiments ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
extends this picture to physical storage on LoCoMo, where we measure the
final $`\mathcal{B}_{t}`$ size of each system under matched backbone and
embedding protocols. By default (A0) PreCoMem keeps deprecated facts as
provenance hooks for traits that may resurface later, since
confidence-weighted retrieval already filters them out of the Top-$`K`$;
the optional A1 configuration ($`w\!<\!0.05`$ prune) trades this
provenance for tighter storage and matches A0 within seed variance
(Appendix [Section C.7](#A3.SS7.SSS0.Px3 "FACT pruning ablation. ‣ C.7 Memory dynamics: footprint, contamination, and retention ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")).
Both O-Mem and EverMemOS commit each interaction through a discrete
decision (Add/Ignore/Update in O-Mem; episodic-trace formation with a
binary $`[t_{\mathrm{start}},t_{\mathrm{end}}]`$ validity in EverMemOS),
so neither maintains a continuous per-item confidence and
$`N_{\mathrm{eff}}`$ is undefined. Even on raw count alone, A1 stores
fewer items than both baselines while still leading on accuracy by
$`+2.6`$ and $`+3.4`$ overall points, so storage remains bounded in both
the soft sense ($`N_{\mathrm{eff}}`$ plateau) and the hard sense. This
suggests the memory gain is not purchased by unchecked accumulation.

## 4 Conclusion

PreCoMem treats long-horizon conversational memory not as a transcript
to be stored, but as confidence-weighted beliefs about the user’s state
that must be continually checked. A reliability-adjusted surprisal
signal routes each turn into MAINTAIN (reinforce supported beliefs),
PROFILE (buffer ambiguous cues as low-confidence hypotheses), or CORRECT
(softly decay contradicted facts). The PROFILE waiting room is the
central design choice: it lets evidence accumulate before commitment, so
the agent stays consistent under noise without freezing under genuine
drift. Across LoCoMo, LongMemEval-S, PersonaMem-v2, and DiMoBench, this
discipline yields the best overall accuracy while keeping memory bounded
over a thousand turns; qualitative case studies
(Appendix [Section C.11](#A3.SS11.SSS0.Px8 "Qualitative case studies beyond accuracy. ‣ C.11 System inspectability, traces, and qualitative analyses ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents"))
show calibrated recommendations beyond retrieval-only baselines. We see
continuous confidence and explicit hypothesis lifecycles as a useful
baseline for long-term memory systems, where what to commit matters as
much as what to recall. Our evaluation covers English dialogue
benchmarks; other languages and modalities are left for future work.
````
