````math
## Appendix A Related work

#### Long-term memory for conversational LLMs.

Early work attacks the fixed context window with externalized
hierarchical or virtual memory \[[51](#bib.bib4), [30](#bib.bib3)\].
More recent systems either organize the store more aggressively or learn
richer write rules. *Structured organizations* include interlinked note
stores with associative links \[[45](#bib.bib17)\], time-aware knowledge
graphs \[[34](#bib.bib26)\], OS-style hierarchical
scheduling \[[23](#bib.bib35), [10](#bib.bib25)\], and dedicated
memory-box layouts that segment a user’s history into typed
shards \[[38](#bib.bib36)\]. *Learning-based update rules* optimize
memory operations against downstream rewards \[[42](#bib.bib23),
[50](#bib.bib24), [46](#bib.bib27)\], while *interpretable triggers*
target a single failure mode such as preference
change \[[37](#bib.bib29)\], redundancy \[[24](#bib.bib31),
[21](#bib.bib32)\], controllable reliance \[[13](#bib.bib30)\], or
affective state \[[27](#bib.bib28)\]. A systematic gap shared by these
systems is the *stability–plasticity* regime: append-only
memories \[[45](#bib.bib17)\] treat every observation as equally valid
and dilute retrieval; overwrite memories compress aggressively but lose
information that later turns out to be relevant \[[2](#bib.bib16)\];
OS-style memories \[[23](#bib.bib35), [10](#bib.bib25)\] schedule access
well but still fall back on importance heuristics for writes; and
reinforcement-trained memories \[[42](#bib.bib23), [50](#bib.bib24),
[46](#bib.bib27)\] face sparse credit assignment, since most reward
signal is several turns downstream of the responsible read/write
decision. PreCoMem is complementary to all of these: it treats the write
path as a single *regime-gated* decision driven by a calibrated
prediction-error signal, instead of relying on recency heuristics or
task-specific reward shaping.

#### Predictive coding and surprisal as write signals.

Predictive coding theory holds that perceptual systems update internal
models when observations deviate from prediction \[[8](#bib.bib10),
[3](#bib.bib18)\], and information theory gives this deviation a precise
scalar form: surprisal \[[19](#bib.bib11), [29](#bib.bib12)\]. The same
logic has long shaped accounts of episodic memory: novel events are
encoded preferentially, and reactivation drives reconsolidation of
related traces \[[33](#bib.bib20), [36](#bib.bib19)\]. Translating this
into long-term conversational memory requires two extra ingredients
absent from prior memory systems. First, surprisal must be *calibrated
against retrieval reliability*, otherwise diffuse recall produces noisy
update signals \[[18](#bib.bib21), [48](#bib.bib22)\]. Second, surprisal
must be *regime-gated*, since consistent, ambiguous, and reliably
contradictory evidence call for qualitatively different memory edits.
PreCoMem operationalizes both ingredients: $`S_{\mathrm{eff}}`$
multiplies raw surprisal by an entropy-based reliability coefficient,
and a two-threshold gate maps the result to MAINTAIN/PROFILE/CORRECT.

#### Benchmarking long-term memory.

LoCoMo \[[28](#bib.bib33)\] provides 1,500$`+`$ questions over
multi-session dialogues with explicit single-hop, multi-hop, temporal,
and open-domain splits. LongMemEval-S \[[43](#bib.bib38)\] tests five
core abilities (information extraction, multi-session reasoning,
knowledge updates, temporal reasoning, abstention) with 500 questions
over chat histories of roughly $`115`$k tokens each.
PersonaMem-v2 \[[16](#bib.bib37)\] stresses *implicit* personalization
across $`1{,}000`$ personas and $`20{,}000+`$ preferences, where most
signal is buried in everyday task-oriented turns. Two more recent
benchmarks move closer to the write path: AMemGym \[[1](#bib.bib48)\]
evaluates memory on-policy against a simulated user whose latent task
state changes across periods, and LoCoMo-Plus \[[22](#bib.bib49)\]
probes implicit cognitive constraints (causal, state, goal, and value)
over long dialogues; we report both in
[Section C.1](#A3.SS1 "C.1 Third-party benchmarks: AMemGym and LoCoMo-Plus ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents").
EvalMem \[[26](#bib.bib51)\] complements these end-task benchmarks with
an operation-level diagnostic framework that scores individual memory
operations such as adding, updating, and deleting items. These
benchmarks probe the read path or the end result of memory updates; none
of them, however, isolates the *write-path* decision under continuous,
gradual drift with adversarial retrieval interference. Our companion
benchmark DiMoBench fills this gap with five independent $`350`$-turn
dialogues, one per persona, whose latent attitudes reverse gradually
across change points; an extended variant, DiMoBench-XL, contains five
independent $`1050`$-turn dialogues, each with three sequential drifts.

## Appendix B Additional method details

### B.1 From expected predictive loss to a per-turn on-policy loss

#### Conceptual predictive loss.

Let $`h_{t}=(u_{\leq t},y_{<t})`$ denote the dialogue history up to turn
$`t`$. The conceptual predictive loss

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{pred}}(t)=\mathbb{E}_{u\sim p(u\mid h_{t})}\!\left[S_{\mathrm{eff}}(u)\right]
``` |  | (11) |

captures the expected effective surprisal under the (unknown)
interaction distribution $`p(u\mid h_{t})`$, and the long-horizon goal
is to update the memory state to reduce future expected losses,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\min_{\mathcal{M}_{t+1}}\;\mathbb{E}\!\left[\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{pred}}(t+k)\mid\mathcal{M}_{t+1}\right],\qquad k\geq 1.
``` |  | (12) |

#### Monte Carlo surrogate.

Because $`p(u\mid h_{t})`$ is generally unknown, under a fixed response
policy we treat the observed turn $`u_{t}`$ as a Monte Carlo sample and
use the estimator

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{pred}}(t):=S_{\mathrm{eff}}(u_{t}).
``` |  | (13) |

Defining the per-turn on-policy loss
$`x_{t}:=\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{pred}}(t)`$ yields an unbiased
estimate

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbb{E}\!\left[x_{t}\mid h_{t}\right]=\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{pred}}(t).
``` |  | (14) |

In the main text we write $`S_{\mathrm{eff}}(u_{t})`$ without explicitly
conditioning on the memory state; implicitly, $`S_{\mathrm{eff}}`$
depends on the retrieved evidence produced by
$`\texttt{Retrieve}(\mathcal{M}_{t},u_{t})`$.

#### Greedy regularized surrogate for memory updates.

The long-horizon objective in
[Equation 12](#A2.E12 "In Conceptual predictive loss. ‣ B.1 From expected predictive loss to a per-turn on-policy loss ‣ Appendix B Additional method details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
can be approximated on-policy by folding “future benefit” into a myopic,
regularized surrogate:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{M}_{t+1}\approx\arg\min_{\mathcal{M}}\;x_{t}(\mathcal{M})+D(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}_{t})+\Omega(\mathcal{M}),
``` |  | (15) |

where $`x_{t}(\mathcal{M})`$ denotes the effective-surprisal loss one
would obtain if evaluated with memory state $`\mathcal{M}`$, $`D`$ is an
edit/stability cost (e.g., penalizing large changes or changes to
high-confidence beliefs), and $`\Omega`$ is a capacity/complexity
penalty that discourages unbounded growth of provisional beliefs.
Concretely, we instantiate $`\Omega`$ with a lightweight pruning rule
for the hypothesis pool: belief items with
$`\tau(m)=\texttt{HYPOTHESIS}`$ are removed once their confidence falls
below a small threshold (we use $`w_{t}(m)<0.1`$), which controls memory
size while retaining only actively supported hypotheses. In contrast,
belief items with $`\tau(m)=\texttt{FACT}`$ are not directly deleted;
due to the one-way promotion scheme in
[Section 2.2](#S2.SS2 "2.2 Memory primitives ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents"),
outdated facts are instead handled by soft-decay correction that reduces
their confidence and thus deprioritizes them under confidence-weighted
retrieval. The Memory Evolution phase instantiates a discrete,
interpretable approximation of
[Equation 15](#A2.E15 "In Greedy regularized surrogate for memory updates. ‣ B.1 From expected predictive loss to a per-turn on-policy loss ‣ Appendix B Additional method details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents"):
action selection corresponds to cost-sensitive choice
([Section B.3](#A2.SS3.SSS0.Px3 "Two-threshold gate. ‣ B.3 Operator and gate derivations ‣ Appendix B Additional method details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")),
while operator updates admit simple optimization views
([Sections B.3](#A2.SS3 "B.3 Operator and gate derivations ‣ Appendix B Additional method details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
and [B.3](#A2.SS3.SSS0.Px2 "CORRECT update. ‣ B.3 Operator and gate derivations ‣ Appendix B Additional method details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")).

#### Why low-confidence facts are retained rather than pruned.

We deliberately apply the $`w_{t}(m)<0.1`$ pruning rule only to
hypotheses, not to facts. Two design considerations motivate this
asymmetry. First, because retrieval is confidence-weighted, an outdated
fact with $`w\!\approx\!0.02`$ contributes roughly 50$`\times`$ less to
the retrieval score than a currently supported fact with
$`w\!\approx\!1`$; such items are naturally pushed out of the Top-$`K`$
evidence set by currently supported beliefs and become *functionally
inactive* under confidence-weighted retrieval even though they remain in
storage. Second, long-horizon dialogue often revisits past user traits
after long gaps (e.g., a user stops drinking coffee for months and then
mentions it again); retaining the deprecated fact at low confidence
preserves the reasoning chain and episodic-cache links needed to recover
such context, whereas hard deletion would force the system to rebuild
that trait from scratch. The resulting *effective memory*, i.e., the
subset of items that can still influence Top-$`K`$ retrieval, therefore
remains controlled even if raw storage accumulates history. Under truly
extreme horizons, raw storage can be further bounded by a timestamped
time-to-live on long-deprecated facts without modifying the core
operators; we leave this as an engineering safeguard rather than a core
mechanism.

### B.2 Retrieval scoring and surprisal component definitions

#### Retrieval scoring.

Let $`\mathbf{e}(\cdot)`$ be a text embedding function and let
$`\mathcal{P}_{t}`$ be the merged candidate pool recalled from
$`\mathcal{B}_{t}`$ and $`\mathcal{E}_{t}`$. We use three reproducible
relevance signals:

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle s_{\mathrm{sem}}(u_{t},m)`$ | $`\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\left(1+\cos(\mathbf{e}(u_{t}),\mathbf{e}(c(m)))\right)\in[0,1],`$ |  | (16) |
|  | $`\displaystyle s_{\mathrm{fact}}(u_{t},m)`$ | $`\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{BM25}(u_{t},c(m))}{\max_{m^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{t}}\mathrm{BM25}(u_{t},c(m^{\prime}))+\epsilon}\in[0,1],`$ |  | (17) |

where $`\epsilon>0`$ is a stability constant. For topical relevance, we
maintain topic prototypes $`\{\mathbf{t}_{z}\}_{z=1}^{Z_{t}}`$ (e.g.,
$`\mathbf{t}_{z}=\mathbf{e}(\ell_{z})`$ for topic description
$`\ell_{z}`$) and define

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle s_{\mathrm{topic}}(u_{t},m)`$ | $`\displaystyle=p(z(m)\mid u_{t}),`$ |  | (18) |
|  | $`\displaystyle p(z\mid x)`$ | $`\displaystyle=\operatorname{softmax}_{z}\!\left(\cos(\mathbf{e}(x),\mathbf{t}_{z})\right).`$ |  |  |

We assign each item a topic label at write time as
$`z(m)=\arg\max_{z}p(z\mid c(m))`$, and $`Z_{t}`$ denotes the number of
topics at turn $`t`$.

#### Surprisal components.

Let $`\hat{s}_{i}`$ be the normalized retrieval weights from
[Equation 3](#S2.E3 "In 2.4 Memory evolution: regime-gated belief updating ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents").
We compute a score-weighted evidence embedding

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbf{e}(\mathcal{C}_{t})=\sum_{i=1}^{K}\hat{s}_{i}\,\mathbf{e}(c(m_{i})),
``` |  | (19) |

and define semantic deviation as a cosine distance:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
d_{\mathrm{sem}}(u_{t},\mathcal{C}_{t})=\tfrac{1}{2}\left(1-\cos(\mathbf{e}(u_{t}),\mathbf{e}(\mathcal{C}_{t}))\right)\in[0,1].
``` |  | (20) |

For contradiction, let

|     |                                                                |     |      |
|-----|----------------------------------------------------------------|-----|------|
|     |
       ``` math
       \pi_{t}(m):=\mathrm{Verif}_{\mathrm{conf}}(u_{t},c(m))\in[0,1]
       ```                                                             |     | (21) |

be the *calibrated* contradiction probability predicted by a fixed NLI
verifier. In all experiments, $`\mathrm{Verif}_{\mathrm{conf}}`$ is
instantiated as the cross-encoder/nli-deberta-v3-base cross-encoder:
given the premise–hypothesis pair $`(c(m),u_{t})`$, let
$`\mathbf{z}(u_{t},c(m))\in\mathbb{R}^{3}`$ denote its logits over
$`\{\text{entail},\text{neutral},\text{contr}\}`$. We fit a scalar
temperature $`T>0`$ on the MultiNLI (MNLI) matched development split and
define
$`\pi_{t}(m)=\operatorname{softmax}(\mathbf{z}(u_{t},c(m))/T)_{\text{contr}}`$,
and set

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
d_{\mathrm{conf}}(u_{t},\mathcal{C}_{t})=\max_{m_{i}\in\mathcal{C}_{t}}\pi_{t}(m_{i})\in[0,1].
``` |  | (22) |

#### Memory item fields.

A memory item $`m`$ is a typed belief node (FACT or HYPOTHESIS) with
content $`c(m)`$, confidence weight $`w_{t}(m)\in[0,1]`$, and the
retrieval metadata listed in
[Section 2.2](#S2.SS2 "2.2 Memory primitives ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents");
the three metadata fields are constructed as follows. *Entities and
keywords* are proposed by the write-path sub-agent at write time (the
Profile-Agent’s evidence_keywords); no separate entity-recognition model
is involved, and they are stored as a structured field that is used only
by the lexical term $`s_{\mathrm{fact}}`$ of
[Equation 1](#S2.E1 "In 2.3 Memory leveraging: evidence recall and response generation ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents").
*Topic labels* are assigned at write time as
$`z(m)=\arg\max_{z}p(z\mid c(m))`$ with the prototypes of
[Equation 18](#A2.E18 "In Retrieval scoring. ‣ B.2 Retrieval scoring and surprisal component definitions ‣ Appendix B Additional method details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents");
the topic table $`\mathcal{T}_{t}`$ keeps one running-mean centroid per
topic and acts only as the topic prior $`s_{\mathrm{topic}}`$. *Dense
embeddings* are computed solely from the belief text,
$`\mathbf{e}(c(m))`$ with Qwen3-Embedding-0.6B (normalized); entity and
topic information never enter the embedding and contribute only through
their own terms. Episodic traces are embedded from their own dialogue
text and serve as provenance only.
[Section C.11](#A3.SS11.SSS0.Px1 "Memory store serialization and example snapshot. ‣ C.11 System inspectability, traces, and qualitative analyses ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
shows a serialized snapshot.

#### Cold start.

When the belief store is still empty, retrieval returns no evidence and
the surprisal terms above are undefined. By convention the first
observations of a user are then handled by PROFILE: they enter the store
as provisional hypotheses that later turns can reinforce, correct, or
let decay, exactly as for a populated store.
[Section C.8](#A3.SS8.SSS0.Px4 "Cold start and genuinely new topics. ‣ C.8 Retrieval dependence and reliability calibration ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
reports how reliably first mentions are written under this convention.

1: $`(u_{t},\,Q_{t-1},\,M_{t})`$ with $`M_{t}=(B_{t},E_{t},T_{t})`$

2: $`(y_{t},\,M_{t+1})`$

3: // Phase A: Memory Leveraging (deterministic)

4: $`(C_{t},s_{t})\leftarrow\textsc{Retrieve}(M_{t},u_{t})`$
$`\triangleright`$
[Equation 1](#S2.E1 "In 2.3 Memory leveraging: evidence recall and response generation ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents"):
$`w_{t}`$-modulated tri-signal

5: $`y_{t}\leftarrow\textsc{Respond}(u_{t},Q_{t-1},C_{t})`$
$`\triangleright`$
[Equation 2](#S2.E2 "In 2.3 Memory leveraging: evidence recall and response generation ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")

6: // Phase B: Reliability + Surprisal (deterministic)

7: $`\rho_{t}\leftarrow 1-\textsc{NormalizedEntropy}(s_{t})`$
$`\triangleright`$
[Equation 3](#S2.E3 "In 2.4 Memory evolution: regime-gated belief updating ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")

8: $`d_{\mathrm{sem}}\leftarrow 0.5\,(1-\cos(e(u_{t}),e(C_{t})))`$
$`\triangleright`$
[Equation 20](#A2.E20 "In Surprisal components. ‣ B.2 Retrieval scoring and surprisal component definitions ‣ Appendix B Additional method details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")

9:
$`d_{\mathrm{conf}}\leftarrow\max_{m\in C_{t}}\textsc{NLI\_contradict}(u_{t},c(m))`$
$`\triangleright`$
[Equation 22](#A2.E22 "In Surprisal components. ‣ B.2 Retrieval scoring and surprisal component definitions ‣ Appendix B Additional method details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")

10:
$`S_{\mathrm{raw}}\leftarrow\lambda\,d_{\mathrm{sem}}+(1-\lambda)\,d_{\mathrm{conf}}`$
$`\triangleright`$
[Equation 4](#S2.E4 "In 2.4 Memory evolution: regime-gated belief updating ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")

11: $`x_{t}\leftarrow\rho_{t}\cdot S_{\mathrm{raw}}`$ $`\triangleright`$
[Equation 5](#S2.E5 "In 2.4 Memory evolution: regime-gated belief updating ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")

12: // Phase C: Regime-gated update (hybrid: agent + formula)

13: if $`x_{t}\leq\theta_{\mathrm{low}}`$ then $`\triangleright`$
MAINTAIN regime

14:
  $`\{(m_{i},\mathrm{evidence\_strength}_{i},\mathrm{refinement}_{i})\}\leftarrow\textsc{MaintainAgent}(C_{t},u_{t},x_{t})`$

15:   for each $`(m_{i},\eta_{i},\mathrm{ref}_{i})`$ do

16:    $`\eta_{t}(m_{i})\leftarrow\mathrm{evidence\_strength}_{i}`$

17:
   $`w_{t+1}(m_{i})\leftarrow w_{t}(m_{i})+\eta_{t}(m_{i})\,(1-w_{t}(m_{i}))`$
$`\triangleright`$
[Equation 7](#S2.E7 "In MAINTAIN (reinforcement). ‣ 2.4 Memory evolution: regime-gated belief updating ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")

18:    if $`\mathrm{ref}_{i}`$ then

19:       AttachRefinement$`(m_{i},\mathrm{ref}_{i})`$

20:    end if

21:   end for

22: else if $`x_{t}\leq\theta_{\mathrm{high}}`$ then $`\triangleright`$
PROFILE regime

23:
  $`(\mathrm{class}_{c},\mathrm{content},q_{t},\mathrm{protect})\leftarrow\textsc{ProfileAgent}(u_{t},C_{t},x_{t})`$
$`\triangleright`$ band $`[w_{\min}^{c},w_{\max}^{c}]`$ from
$`\mathrm{class}_{c}`$

24:
  $`h\leftarrow\textsc{NewHypothesis}(\mathrm{content},\ \mathrm{protect})`$

25:
  $`b_{t}\leftarrow w_{\min}^{c}+\sigma(x_{t})\,(w_{\max}^{c}-w_{\min}^{c})`$; $`w_{0}(h)\leftarrow\tfrac{1}{2}\,b_{t}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\mathrm{clip}(q_{t},[w_{\min}^{c},w_{\max}^{c}])`$
$`\triangleright`$
[Equation 8](#S2.E8 "In PROFILE (hypothesis writing). ‣ 2.4 Memory evolution: regime-gated belief updating ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")

26:   $`B_{t+1}\leftarrow B_{t}\cup\{h\}`$

27: else$`\triangleright`$ CORRECT regime

28:
  $`\{(m_{i},\mathrm{severity}_{i},\mathrm{op}_{i})\}\leftarrow\textsc{CorrectAgent}(C_{t},u_{t},x_{t})`$

29:   for each $`(m_{i},\mathrm{severity}_{i})`$ do

30:    $`\zeta_{t}(m_{i})\leftarrow\mathrm{severity}_{i}`$

31:
   $`w_{t+1}(m_{i})\leftarrow w_{t}(m_{i})\,\exp(-\beta\,x_{t}\,\zeta_{t}(m_{i}))`$
$`\triangleright`$
[Equation 10](#S2.E10 "In CORRECT (soft correction). ‣ 2.4 Memory evolution: regime-gated belief updating ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")

32:   end for

33:   Record the new observation as a fresh belief node.

34: end if

35: // Phase D: Hypothesis lifecycle + write-back (deterministic)

36: for each $`h\in B_{t+1}`$ with $`\tau(h)=\textsc{Hypothesis}`$ do

37:   if $`h`$ not protected and not supported this turn then

38:    $`w_{t}(h)\leftarrow w_{t}(h)\,\exp(-\gamma\,a_{t}(h))`$,
$`a_{t}(h)=t-\ell(h)`$ $`\triangleright`$
[Equation 9](#S2.E9 "In PROFILE (hypothesis writing). ‣ 2.4 Memory evolution: regime-gated belief updating ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")

39:   else

40:    $`\ell(h)\leftarrow t`$ if supported this turn $`\triangleright`$
reset anchor, skip decay

41:   end if

42:   if $`w_{t}(h)\geq\tau_{\mathrm{promote}}`$ then

43:    $`\tau(h)\leftarrow\textsc{Fact}`$

44:   end if

45:   if $`w_{t}(h)<0.1`$ then

46:    Prune $`h`$

47:   end if

48: end for

49: $`E_{t+1}\leftarrow E_{t}\cup\{(u_{t},y_{t})\}`$ $`\triangleright`$
episodic write-back

Algorithm 1 PreCoMem per-turn pipeline (hybrid execution). Deterministic
steps (retrieval, $`\rho_{t}`$, $`S_{\mathrm{raw}}`$, gate, $`w`$
updates, lifecycle) are interleaved with three regime-specific LLM
sub-agents that supply per-item targets and bounded scalar scores.
Equation references point to the main text and, for $`d_{\mathrm{sem}}`$
and $`d_{\mathrm{conf}}`$, to
[Section B.2](#A2.SS2 "B.2 Retrieval scoring and surprisal component definitions ‣ Appendix B Additional method details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents").

### B.3 Operator and gate derivations

#### MAINTAIN update.

The MAINTAIN update in
[Equation 7](#S2.E7 "In MAINTAIN (reinforcement). ‣ 2.4 Memory evolution: regime-gated belief updating ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
is a single gradient-descent step on the convex consolidation loss
$`\ell^{+}(w)=\tfrac{1}{2}(1-w)^{2}`$. For a scalar weight $`w`$,

|     |                                                              |     |
|-----|--------------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       w_{t+1}=w_{t}-\eta\nabla\ell^{+}(w_{t})=w_{t}+\eta(1-w_{t}),
       ```                                                           |     |

which matches
[Equation 7](#S2.E7 "In MAINTAIN (reinforcement). ‣ 2.4 Memory evolution: regime-gated belief updating ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents").
If $`w_{t}\in[0,1]`$ and $`\eta\in(0,1]`$, then $`w_{t+1}\in[0,1]`$ and
the residual shrinks as $`1-w_{t+1}=(1-\eta)(1-w_{t})`$.

#### CORRECT update.

For a retrieved belief item $`m`$, let $`x_{t}=S_{\mathrm{eff}}(u_{t})`$
denote the on-policy loss and let $`\zeta_{t}(m)\in[0,1]`$ be the
per-item severity returned by the Correct-Agent
([Appendix D](#A4 "Appendix D Prompts ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents"));
operationally $`\zeta_{t}(m)`$ plays the role of a context-aware
contradiction weight, and reduces to a calibrated NLI score
$`\pi_{t}(m)`$ when the agent is replaced by the fixed verifier in
[Equation 21](#A2.E21 "In Surprisal components. ‣ B.2 Retrieval scoring and surprisal component definitions ‣ Appendix B Additional method details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents").
Consider the KL-proximal mirror-descent update:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
w_{t+1}=\arg\min_{w\geq 0}\;x_{t}\,\zeta_{t}(m)\,w+\tfrac{1}{\beta}\mathrm{KL}(w\|w_{t}),
``` |  | (23) |

where for scalar weights,

|     |                                                     |     |      |
|-----|-----------------------------------------------------|-----|------|
|     |
       ``` math
       \mathrm{KL}(w\|w_{t})=w\log\frac{w}{w_{t}}-w+w_{t}.
       ```                                                  |     | (24) |

Taking the first-order optimality condition of
[Equation 23](#A2.E23 "In CORRECT update. ‣ B.3 Operator and gate derivations ‣ Appendix B Additional method details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
gives
$`x_{t}\,\zeta_{t}(m)+\tfrac{1}{\beta}\log\frac{w_{t+1}}{w_{t}}=0`$,
hence

|     |                                                             |     |      |
|-----|-------------------------------------------------------------|-----|------|
|     |
       ``` math
       w_{t+1}=w_{t}\exp\!\left(-\beta x_{t}\,\zeta_{t}(m)\right),
       ```                                                          |     | (25) |

which matches
[Equation 10](#S2.E10 "In CORRECT (soft correction). ‣ 2.4 Memory evolution: regime-gated belief updating ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents").

#### Two-threshold gate.

We interpret the gate in
[Equation 6](#S2.E6 "In 2.4 Memory evolution: regime-gated belief updating ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
as selecting an action that minimizes an on-policy risk plus an action
cost:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
a_{t}=\arg\min_{a\in\{\mathrm{M,P,C}\}}\widehat{J}_{t}(a),\qquad\widehat{J}_{t}(a)=\widehat{L}_{t}(a)+c(a).
``` |  | (26) |

Using the proxy $`\widehat{L}_{t}(a)=x_{t}-\Delta_{a}(x_{t})`$ with a
linear improvement model $`\Delta_{a}(x)\approx g_{a}x`$ yields

|     |                                               |     |
|-----|-----------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \widehat{J}_{t}(a)\approx(1-g_{a})x_{t}+c(a).
       ```                                            |     |

The action boundaries are given by pairwise equality of these lines:
$`\widehat{J}_{t}(\mathrm{P})=\widehat{J}_{t}(\mathrm{M})`$ gives

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
x_{t}=\frac{c(\mathrm{P})-c(\mathrm{M})}{g_{\mathrm{P}}-g_{\mathrm{M}}}=:\theta_{\mathrm{low}},
``` |  |

and $`\widehat{J}_{t}(\mathrm{C})=\widehat{J}_{t}(\mathrm{P})`$ gives

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
x_{t}=\frac{c(\mathrm{C})-c(\mathrm{P})}{g_{\mathrm{C}}-g_{\mathrm{P}}}=:\theta_{\mathrm{high}},
``` |  |

which gives the two thresholds:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\theta_{\mathrm{low}}=\frac{c(\mathrm{P})-c(\mathrm{M})}{g_{\mathrm{P}}-g_{\mathrm{M}}},\qquad\theta_{\mathrm{high}}=\frac{c(\mathrm{C})-c(\mathrm{P})}{g_{\mathrm{C}}-g_{\mathrm{P}}}.
``` |  | (27) |

We treat $`(c(a),g_{a})`$ as interpretable design abstractions rather
than quantities to be estimated end-to-end: they motivate a
*two-threshold* rule and explain why the boundaries should be ordered,
but the operating point
$`(\theta_{\mathrm{low}},\theta_{\mathrm{high}})=(0.2,0.6)`$ is
calibrated empirically via the sensitivity sweep in
[Section C.5](#A3.SS5.SSS0.Px6 "Sensitivity to gating thresholds. ‣ C.5 Hyperparameter sensitivity and adaptive variants ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents"),
and a personalized data-driven instantiation is given in
[Section C.5](#A3.SS5.SSS0.Px7 "Adaptive thresholding for personalization. ‣ C.5 Hyperparameter sensitivity and adaptive variants ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents").

#### PROFILE initialization.

[Equation 8](#S2.E8 "In PROFILE (hypothesis writing). ‣ 2.4 Memory evolution: regime-gated belief updating ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
fuses a gate-level prior with the agent’s scalar. The class label $`c`$
returned by the Profile-Agent selects the band
$`I_{c}=[w_{\min}^{(c)},w_{\max}^{(c)}]`$; the deterministic layer
computes the prior
$`b_{t}^{(c)}=w_{\min}^{(c)}+\sigma(x_{t})\bigl(w_{\max}^{(c)}-w_{\min}^{(c)}\bigr)`$,
clips the agent’s initial_confidence $`q_{t}`$ to $`I_{c}`$, and
averages the two. For example, with $`I_{c}=[0.60,0.70]`$, $`x_{t}=0.4`$
and $`q_{t}=0.65`$, one obtains $`b_{t}^{(c)}=0.6599`$ and
$`w_{0}(h)=0.6549`$. Because $`q_{t}`$ is clipped before it is halved,
two agent scalars that differ by the full band width move $`w_{0}`$ by
at most $`0.05`$, so the number of MAINTAIN updates needed for promotion
under
[Equation 7](#S2.E7 "In MAINTAIN (reinforcement). ‣ 2.4 Memory evolution: regime-gated belief updating ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
changes by at most one, and $`w_{\max}^{(c)}<\tau_{\mathrm{promote}}`$
guarantees that no single agent output can commit a hypothesis as a
FACT. The narrow range of $`\sigma(x_{t})`$ inside the band is therefore
intentional: the class band carries the semantics, and the within-band
value only fine-tunes the starting point. The agent additionally returns
the decay_protection flag that governs
[Equation 9](#S2.E9 "In PROFILE (hypothesis writing). ‣ 2.4 Memory evolution: regime-gated belief updating ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents").

#### Hypothesis decay horizon.

[Equation 9](#S2.E9 "In PROFILE (hypothesis writing). ‣ 2.4 Memory evolution: regime-gated belief updating ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
is applied once per turn to every unprotected hypothesis that received
no supporting evidence in that turn, with the exponent $`a_{t}(h)`$
measured from the last evidence-bearing update $`\ell(h)`$. For a
hypothesis that is never reinforced the factors compound, so after $`n`$
consecutive unsupported turns

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
w_{\ell+n}(h)=w_{\ell}(h)\exp\!\Bigl(-\gamma\,\frac{n(n+1)}{2}\Bigr),
``` |  | (28) |

which is faster than a fixed per-turn factor. This is intended: it
instantiates the capacity penalty $`\Omega`$ of
[Equation 15](#A2.E15 "In Greedy regularized surrogate for memory updates. ‣ B.1 From expected predictive loss to a per-turn on-policy loss ‣ Appendix B Additional method details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
(hypotheses are pruned below $`w=0.1`$) and is why effective memory
plateaus over the dialogue
([Section 3.5](#S3.SS5 "3.5 Memory scalability ‣ 3 Experiments ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")).
With the decay rate $`\gamma=0.05`$ used in our experiments, an
unsupported latent hypothesis started inside its band is pruned after
eight consecutive unsupported turns. The horizon counts only consecutive
unsupported turns: a single reinforcement resets $`\ell(h)`$ and
restarts the window, and explicit-event hypotheses are decay-protected,
so only guesses that are never revalidated are culled.

### B.4 Per-turn pipeline (hybrid execution)

Algorithm [1](#alg1 "Algorithm 1 ‣ Cold start. ‣ B.2 Retrieval scoring and surprisal component definitions ‣ Appendix B Additional method details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
makes the hybrid execution discussed in
[Section 2.4](#S2.SS4 "2.4 Memory evolution: regime-gated belief updating ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
explicit. Each turn invokes (i) a deterministic Retrieve step, (ii) a
deterministic computation of $`\rho_{t},S_{\mathrm{raw}},x_{t}`$, (iii)
a regime-specific LLM sub-agent (one of Maintain-Agent, Profile-Agent,
Correct-Agent; prompts in
[Appendix D](#A4 "Appendix D Prompts ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents"))
that emits per-item scalar scores, and (iv) a deterministic application
of the closed-form updates in
[Equations 7](#S2.E7 "In MAINTAIN (reinforcement). ‣ 2.4 Memory evolution: regime-gated belief updating ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents"),
[8](#S2.E8 "Equation 8 ‣ PROFILE (hypothesis writing). ‣ 2.4 Memory evolution: regime-gated belief updating ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
and [10](#S2.E10 "Equation 10 ‣ CORRECT (soft correction). ‣ 2.4 Memory evolution: regime-gated belief updating ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents").
The sub-agents only inject *which* items to touch and *how strongly*;
gating, soft-decay, reinforcement, and hypothesis-promotion mechanics
are entirely symbolic.

## Appendix C Additional experimental details

### C.1 Third-party benchmarks: AMemGym and LoCoMo-Plus

To test whether the write-path advantage transfers to data we did not
design, we evaluate PreCoMem on two public benchmarks built by other
groups: AMemGym \[[1](#bib.bib48)\], an on-policy benchmark with gold
state transitions in which a simulated user’s latent task state (for
example a project’s preparation stage or budget band) changes across
periods, and LoCoMo-Plus \[[22](#bib.bib49)\], a static benchmark of 401
implicit cognitive-constraint questions. Both are run under their
official protocols with the same backbone for all compared systems.

#### AMemGym.

We use the official on-policy v1.base release (code snapshot ffcd188,
data 4b8f64f) at the official 20-profile / 11-period setting (ten gold
state transitions per profile; 943 sessions and 2,200 questions per run)
with three paired seeds $`(13,42,1234)`$. The user simulator is gpt-4.1
($`T{=}0.2`$); PreCoMem and all of its variants use gpt-4.1-mini
($`T{=}0`$) with text-embedding-3-small, Top-$`K{=}10`$,
$`(\theta_{\mathrm{low}},\theta_{\mathrm{high}})=(0.2,0.6)`$ and
$`\lambda=0.5`$. Each AMemGym session is a single user turn, so one gate
event is logged per session. We report the benchmark’s two headline
scores, the Overall accuracy over the 2,200 questions and the normalized
Memory score defined by AMemGym (Overall rescaled between the
benchmark’s random-answer and upper-bound references), together with its
native write / read / utilization failure rates.
[Table 3](#A3.T3 "In AMemGym. ‣ C.1 Third-party benchmarks: AMemGym and LoCoMo-Plus ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
compares PreCoMem with the strongest systems reported in the AMemGym
paper under the same gpt-4.1-mini backbone: PreCoMem leads the strongest
of them (AWE) by $`+0.028`$ Overall and $`+0.033`$ Memory, so the
advantage reproduces on data we did not design.

| System                       | Overall $`\uparrow`$ | Memory $`\uparrow`$ |
|------------------------------|----------------------|---------------------|
| No memory (gpt-4.1-mini)^(†) | 0.368                | 0.203               |
| A-Mem^(†)                    | 0.378                | 0.220               |
| Nemori^(†)                   | 0.385                | 0.231               |
| Mem0-Graph^(†)               | 0.424                | 0.284               |
| AWE^(†)                      | 0.430                | 0.296               |
| PreCoMem                     | 0.458 $`\pm`$ 0.006  | 0.329 $`\pm`$ 0.009 |

Table 3: AMemGym (official v1.base, 20 profiles $`\times`$ 11 periods,
gpt-4.1-mini for all memory systems). Overall accuracy and normalized
Memory score; PreCoMem is mean $`\pm`$ std over three seeds. Rows marked
$`\dagger`$ are the values reported in the AMemGym paper under the same
backbone; AWE was additionally run locally only for the probe metrics
quoted in the text.

To see which operator carries the gain, we remove each operator in turn
on the same runs
([Table 4](#A3.T4 "In AMemGym. ‣ C.1 Third-party benchmarks: AMemGym and LoCoMo-Plus ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")).
Besides the native scores we track, per seed, 203 probes that query an
attribute in the first period after its transition (changed-state
probes) and 397 probes on unchanged attributes (stable-state probes).
*Changed-state* and *stable-state accuracy* are the accuracies on these
two probe sets; *first-write* is the share of changed-state probes for
which the belief that dominates retrieval already encodes the new value;
*stale-state error* is the share of changed-state probes answered with
the superseded value; *recovery* is the mean number of periods until a
changed-state probe that was not immediately correct is answered
correctly; and *old-state suppression* is the share of changed-state
probes for which the superseded belief is absent from the answering
Top-10 or ranked below the new one. The three operators leave three
distinct signatures, matching
[Figure 4](#S3.F4 "In 3.3 Ablation studies and belief-drift adaptation ‣ 3 Experiments ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")(c):
removing PROFILE mainly costs first-write success and changed-state
accuracy; removing CORRECT raises the stale-state error, stretches
recovery from one period to three, and degrades old-state suppression
the most; removing MAINTAIN degrades stable-state accuracy together with
the highest read and utilization failure. Paired 95% confidence
intervals of Full minus each variant (three seeds, $`t`$-distribution)
exclude zero on every metric; for the Memory score they are
$`0.020\,[0.011,0.029]`$, $`0.026\,[0.012,0.040]`$ and
$`0.056\,[0.040,0.072]`$ for the three variants. Under the same probes,
our local run of AWE gives a stale-state error of $`15.6\%`$ and a
recovery of $`2.0`$ periods, against $`10.8\%`$ and $`1.0`$ for
PreCoMem. In w/o PROFILE, medium-surprisal observations bypass the
waiting room and are committed directly as beliefs; this is the same
variant as in
[Figure 4](#S3.F4 "In 3.3 Ablation studies and belief-drift adaptation ‣ 3 Experiments ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")(c)
and
[Table 5](#A3.T5 "In LoCoMo-Plus. ‣ C.1 Third-party benchmarks: AMemGym and LoCoMo-Plus ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents").

Operator ablations of this kind need a gold transition timeline that
splits accuracy into stable, change-point and recovery phases. Static
question-answering benchmarks such as LoCoMo and LongMemEval-S yield a
single end number per run and cannot attribute an effect to one
operator, which is why we report operator ablations on AMemGym and
DiMoBench and complement them with the route-matched controls of
[Section C.4](#A3.SS4.SSS0.Px5 "Route-matched and factorial controls. ‣ C.4 Component, operator, and control ablations ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents").

| Variant | Overall$`\uparrow`$ | Memory$`\uparrow`$ | Write fail.$`\downarrow`$ | Read fail.$`\downarrow`$ | Util. fail.$`\downarrow`$ |
|----|----|----|----|----|----|
| Full PreCoMem | 0.458 | 0.329 | .300 | .170 | .072 |
| w/o PROFILE | 0.444 | 0.309 | .320 | .165 | .071 |
| w/o CORRECT | 0.439 | 0.303 | .312 | .178 | .071 |
| w/o MAINTAIN | 0.418 | 0.273 | .310 | .190 | .082 |

| Variant | Changed-state$`\uparrow`$ | First-write$`\uparrow`$ | Stale-state$`\downarrow`$ | Recovery (periods)$`\downarrow`$ | Stable-state$`\uparrow`$ | Old-state suppr.$`\uparrow`$ |
|----|----|----|----|----|----|----|
| Full PreCoMem | 0.704 | 76.4% | 10.8% | 1.0 | 0.762 | 83.1% |
| w/o PROFILE | 0.654 | 67.8% | 14.4% | 1.8 | 0.746 | 74.5% |
| w/o CORRECT | 0.631 | 70.1% | 19.2% | 3.0 | 0.745 | 64.2% |
| w/o MAINTAIN | 0.586 | 65.5% | 21.5% | 3.2 | 0.704 | 76.5% |

Table 4: Operator ablation on AMemGym (mean over three seeds). Top: the
benchmark’s native scores and failure rates (the three failure rates sum
to $`1-`$Overall). Bottom: the state-query probes defined in the text
(203 changed-state and 397 stable-state probes per seed). Paired 95% CIs
of Full minus each variant exclude zero on every metric.

#### LoCoMo-Plus.

LoCoMo-Plus contains 401 questions that probe implicit cognitive
constraints of four types (causal 101, state 100, goal 100, value 100)
and scores each answer by constraint-consistency accuracy (%), averaged
over repeated judgments so that scores are not integer counts. All
systems answer with GPT-4o. PreCoMem reaches $`22.4\pm 0.8`$ overall,
above A-Mem ($`17.2`$), Mem0 ($`15.8`$), SeCom \[[31](#bib.bib50)\]
($`14.9`$) and even full-context GPT-4o ($`21.05`$), as reported in the
LoCoMo-Plus paper.
[Table 5](#A3.T5 "In LoCoMo-Plus. ‣ C.1 Third-party benchmarks: AMemGym and LoCoMo-Plus ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
breaks the score down by type against the w/o PROFILE control: the gains
are largest on the non-preference types goal ($`+5.0`$) and state
($`+4.8`$), smaller on value ($`+3.0`$), and within seed noise on causal
($`+1.1\pm 0.5`$), whose questions depend more on backbone reasoning
than on the write path. Together with the task-state targets tracked by
AMemGym, this shows the mechanism operating beyond preference modeling:
nothing in the effective surprisal, the gate or the closed-form updates
is preference-specific, since a belief is any compact claim carrying a
confidence weight.

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
| Type | $`n`$ | PreCoMem | w/o PROFILE | $`\Delta`$ |
| goal | 100 | 22.3 $`\pm`$ 1.1 | 17.3 $`\pm`$ 2.0 | $`+5.0`$ |
| state | 100 | 22.6 $`\pm`$ 0.9 | 17.8 $`\pm`$ 1.2 | $`+4.8`$ |
| value | 100 | 21.6 $`\pm`$ 0.8 | 18.6 $`\pm`$ 0.9 | $`+3.0`$ |
| causal | 101 | 23.0 $`\pm`$ 0.6 | 21.9 $`\pm`$ 1.0 | $`+1.1`$ |
| Overall | 401 | 22.4 $`\pm`$ 0.8 | 18.9 $`\pm`$ 1.2 | $`+3.5`$ |
| Reported overall baselines: full-context GPT-4o 21.05; A-Mem 17.2; Mem0 15.8; SeCom 14.9 |  |  |  |  |

Table 5: LoCoMo-Plus by constraint type (constraint-consistency
accuracy, %, GPT-4o for all systems; mean $`\pm`$ std over three seeds;
Overall is the question-weighted aggregate over the 401 questions).
Baseline overall scores are those reported in the LoCoMo-Plus paper.

### C.2 Backbone-generalization protocol and token usage

#### Scale-vs-mechanism protocol behind [Figure 3](#S2.F3 "In Trace and topic write-back. ‣ 2.4 Memory evolution: regime-gated belief updating ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")(a)–(d).

Each x-axis group of
[Figure 3](#S2.F3 "In Trace and topic write-back. ‣ 2.4 Memory evolution: regime-gated belief updating ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")(a)–(d)
fixes the model that answers the questions: Qwen3.5-35B, Qwen3.5-122B or
GPT-5-Chat. The two bars of a group differ only in how memory is built
and maintained. The left bar runs the answer model with its own native
memory, instantiated by O-Mem at the same scale, so every such bar
already includes a full memory system. The right bar keeps the answer
model unchanged and delegates memory construction (the write path and
retrieval) to PreCoMem running on a one-tier-smaller backbone:
Qwen3.5-9B for the Qwen3.5-35B group, Qwen3.5-35B for the Qwen3.5-122B
group, and GPT-5-mini for the GPT-5-Chat group. The right bar exceeds
the left one in every group and on every dataset (all twelve
$`\Delta>0`$): memory construction can be delegated to a much cheaper
model and still beat the larger model’s native memory, i.e., with the
answer model held equal the mechanism substitutes for roughly one tier
of model scale.

#### Token usage across benchmarks.

[Table 6](#A3.T6 "In Token usage across benchmarks. ‣ C.2 Backbone-generalization protocol and token usage ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
reports the per-query token usage of the response model on all three
benchmarks under the same accounting as the Avg. Tokens column of
[Table 1](#S2.T1 "In Trace and topic write-back. ‣ 2.4 Memory evolution: regime-gated belief updating ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents"):
the mean number of input tokens sent to the response model per query
(retrieved memory, short-term window, question and instructions),
excluding memory-construction calls. PreCoMem’s prompt is bounded by
construction (Top-$`K{=}10`$ beliefs, a six-turn window and at most
eight episodic excerpts), so its budget grows only from 3.1k on LoCoMo
to 3.8k on LongMemEval-S under gpt-4.1, and by under 9% when the
PersonaMem-v2 context quadruples from 32k to 128k. On every benchmark no
baseline is both cheaper and more accurate than PreCoMem, while MemOS
and EverMemOS use 0.6–1.4k fewer tokens per query at lower accuracy.

#### LLM-as-a-judge validation across benchmarks.

To validate the reliability of our automated evaluation, we conduct a
per-category human–LLM agreement study on all three benchmarks.
[Table 8](#A3.T8 "In LLM-as-a-judge validation across benchmarks. ‣ C.3 Extended results and judge validation ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
pools the results: across LoCoMo (199-QA stratified audit),
LongMemEval-S (full 500 QAs), and DiMoBench (200-QA audit covering one
canonical persona), the gpt-4.1-mini primary judge agrees with expert
human re-annotation at $`96.9\%`$ overall ($`93.8`$–$`98.5\%`$ per
question type) with Cohen’s $`\kappa`$ between $`0.81`$ and $`0.96`$ in
every question type ($`0.957`$, $`0.878`$ and $`0.821`$ at the dataset
level for LoCoMo, LongMemEval-S and DiMoBench). $`\kappa`$ is lowest on
DiMoBench, which combines the lowest agreement ($`95.4\%`$) with a high
chance-agreement baseline: both raters mark about 85% of its answers
correct, so more of the agreement is expected by chance than on LoCoMo,
where about 72% are marked correct. To rule out the concern that the
judge might be calibrated only on PreCoMem outputs, we additionally
re-ran the same human-vs-judge study on $`50`$ outputs each from the two
strongest baselines (EverMemOS and TiMem) on LoCoMo and LongMemEval-S;
agreement remained within a $`\pm 0.7`$-point band of the pooled
PreCoMem agreement ($`96.4`$–$`97.5\%`$), confirming that the protocol
is stable across systems. This supports the use of LLM-as-a-judge in our
experiments.

#### Hypothesis lifecycle statistics.

![Refer to caption](2610.29467v1/hypo.png)

Figure 6: Hypothesis lifecycle under PreCoMem. A hypothesis created by
PROFILE (Stage 1) enters multi-turn verification (Stage 2): consistent
support raises its confidence toward the promotion threshold
$`\tau_{\mathrm{promote}}`$ (Stage 3 $`\to`$ Stage 4, becoming a stable
core-profile belief that ranks above hypotheses in confidence-weighted
retrieval), while no/weak support lets its weight decay; once $`w_{t}`$
falls below the prune threshold the hypothesis is removed from the
active pool (Stage 5a); a committed FACT that is later reliably
contradicted is instead softly decayed and kept for provenance (Stage
5b). The horizontal axis is confidence, from low confidence through the
promotion threshold to stable high confidence.

To make self-evolution *observable*, we treat hypotheses as lifecycle
objects that can be created, implicitly verified, promoted, or expired
(illustrated in
[Figure 6](#A3.F6 "In Hypothesis lifecycle statistics. ‣ C.3 Extended results and judge validation ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")).
[Table 9](#A3.T9 "In LLM-as-a-judge validation across benchmarks. ‣ C.3 Extended results and judge validation ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
reports aggregate statistics on LoCoMo (averaged over 10 samples).
Removing retrieval-reliability adjustment (w/o C1) increases hypothesis
creation but reduces promotion precision, while removing the
intermediate PROFILE regime (w/o C2) weakens gradual adaptation. Without
the hypothesis lifecycle (w/o C3), these lifecycle metrics become
undefined, since medium-surprisal information is written as facts
directly.

#### Category-level annotation supporting the attribution.

The attribution of the gains in the main text rests on the benchmark
annotations. On LoCoMo, Open-Domain is the only split whose questions
are inferential in form (20.8% begin with “Would/Might/What would…”) and
whose gold answers are hedged inferences (14.6%); every other split
scores 0.0% on both. Its target is typically a state not stated in a
single turn: 49.0% of its questions carry two or more evidence pointers
and 32.3% span two or more sessions (mean gap 4.51 sessions). Session
span alone does not explain this: Multi-Hop spans more sessions (10.27
on average) yet has no hedged answers, since it joins co-valid stated
facts, whereas Open-Domain must infer and revise a latent state.
Temporal resolves from a single evidence pointer in 87.5% of cases,
which is why no gain appears there. On LongMemEval-S, all 78 KU
questions have exactly two answer sessions, an old value and the value
superseding it, so an un-updated memory is wrong by construction; SSA
questions instead have one answer session whose gold evidence lies in
assistant turns (51 of 56), a single target among $`48.9`$ sessions on
average, so the SSA gain comes from confidence-weighted retrieval acting
as an implicit reranker. We therefore attribute the drift-related gains
to KU, SSP, cross-session MS and Open-Domain, and describe SSA as
selective memory use.

### C.4 Component, operator, and control ablations

#### Fine-grained ablation of raw-surprisal components.

The raw-surprisal term
$`S_{\mathrm{raw}}=\lambda\,d_{\mathrm{sem}}+(1-\lambda)\,d_{\mathrm{conf}}`$
mixes a semantic-gap signal and a verifier-based contradiction signal
([Equation 4](#S2.E4 "In 2.4 Memory evolution: regime-gated belief updating ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents"),
[Section B.2](#A2.SS2.SSS0.Px2 "Surprisal components. ‣ B.2 Retrieval scoring and surprisal component definitions ‣ Appendix B Additional method details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")).
To disentangle their individual contributions, we ablate each component
by forcing one branch to zero. w/o $`d_{\mathrm{sem}}`$ sets
$`\lambda\!=\!0`$, keeping only the contradiction verifier; w/o
$`d_{\mathrm{conf}}`$ sets $`\lambda\!=\!1`$, keeping only the semantic
gap. As shown in
[Table 10](#A3.T10 "In Fine-grained ablation of raw-surprisal components. ‣ C.4 Component, operator, and control ablations ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents"),
both components are useful, but $`d_{\mathrm{conf}}`$ contributes
substantially more: removing it costs $`-10.08`$ points on LoCoMo and
$`-11.57`$ points on LongMemEval-S, roughly double the drop from
removing $`d_{\mathrm{sem}}`$ ($`-5.74`$ and $`-6.63`$ points
respectively). This matches the design intuition: the semantic gap alone
cannot distinguish a genuinely contradictory utterance from one that is
merely off-topic relative to current memory, whereas the NLI-based
contradiction score directly detects reliable negation and drives
CORRECT. Keeping both signals, on the other hand, gives the best
trade-off between sensitivity (detecting drift cues via
$`d_{\mathrm{sem}}`$) and precision (avoiding spurious corrections via
$`d_{\mathrm{conf}}`$).

| Variant | LoCoMo | LongMemEval-S |
|----|----|----|
| Full PreCoMem | 73.57 | 84.28 |
| w/o $`d_{\mathrm{sem}}`$ ($`\lambda{=}0`$) | 67.83 ($`-5.74`$) | 77.65 ($`-6.63`$) |
| w/o $`d_{\mathrm{conf}}`$ ($`\lambda{=}1`$) | 63.49 ($`-10.08`$) | 72.71 ($`-11.57`$) |

Table 10: Fine-grained ablation of the raw-surprisal components.
Accuracy (%) on LoCoMo and LongMemEval-S (gpt-4o-mini backbone). w/o
$`d_{\mathrm{sem}}`$ ($`\lambda{=}0`$) keeps only the NLI-based
contradiction signal; w/o $`d_{\mathrm{conf}}`$ ($`\lambda{=}1`$) keeps
only the semantic-gap signal. Both signals matter, but
$`d_{\mathrm{conf}}`$ contributes nearly twice as much because it
directly detects reliable negation.

#### NLI verifier robustness (sanity check).

Because $`d_{\mathrm{conf}}`$ uses a $`\max`$ statistic over
$`\pi_{t}(m)`$, a natural concern is whether the system is over-reliant
on the specific NLI model behind $`\mathrm{Verif}_{\mathrm{conf}}`$. We
probe this by replacing the default cross-encoder/nli-deberta-v3-base
with two alternative MNLI-trained verifiers (each re-calibrated with a
fresh temperature on the MNLI matched dev split, leaving every other
PreCoMem component untouched) and one *random*-$`\pi_{t}`$ control that
draws $`\pi_{t}(m)\!\sim\!\mathrm{Beta}(2,5)`$ independently per item.
To keep the sweep tractable we use LoCoMo Sample 1 ($`400`$ turns,
$`105`$ QAs; the same sample as the threshold-sensitivity study in
[Section C.5](#A3.SS5.SSS0.Px6 "Sensitivity to gating thresholds. ‣ C.5 Hyperparameter sensitivity and adaptive variants ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents"))
under the gpt-4o-mini backbone, three seeds.

| $`\mathrm{Verif}_{\mathrm{conf}}`$ | Sample-1 Overall | $`\Delta`$ |
|----|----|----|
| cross-encoder/nli-deberta-v3-base (default) | $`76.19\,\pm\,0.42`$ | – |
| facebook/bart-large-mnli | $`75.74\,\pm\,0.38`$ | $`-0.45`$ |
| roberta-large-mnli | $`75.40\,\pm\,0.51`$ | $`-0.79`$ |
| random $`\pi_{t}\!\sim\!\mathrm{Beta}(2,5)`$ (control) | $`65.08\,\pm\,0.79`$ | $`-11.11`$ |

Table 11: NLI verifier sanity check on LoCoMo Sample 1. Sample-1
LLM-judge accuracy (%) when $`\mathrm{Verif}_{\mathrm{conf}}`$ is
replaced. The two alternative MNLI-trained verifiers stay within $`1`$
point of the default; a randomized control collapses by $`11`$ points,
confirming that $`d_{\mathrm{conf}}`$ is load-bearing yet that PreCoMem
is not coupled to a specific verifier checkpoint.

[Table 11](#A3.T11 "In NLI verifier robustness (sanity check). ‣ C.4 Component, operator, and control ablations ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
shows that both alternative NLI checkpoints stay within a $`\pm 1`$
point band of the default; a $`\mathrm{Beta}(2,5)`$-randomized
$`\pi_{t}`$, in contrast, costs $`11`$ points, slightly more than
removing $`d_{\mathrm{conf}}`$ altogether ($`-11.11`$ against $`-10.08`$
in
[Table 10](#A3.T10 "In Fine-grained ablation of raw-surprisal components. ‣ C.4 Component, operator, and control ablations ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")).
We read this as a two-sided sanity check: the $`\max`$-based
$`d_{\mathrm{conf}}`$ is genuinely informative (random replacement is
much worse than dropping the signal), but the gate is robust to the
precise verifier as long as it is calibrated on a standard NLI
distribution. Note that the absolute accuracies in this table are on a
single $`105`$-QA sample and therefore not comparable to the full-LoCoMo
numbers in
[Table 1](#S2.T1 "In Trace and topic write-back. ‣ 2.4 Memory evolution: regime-gated belief updating ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents");
the comparison is a within-row $`\Delta`$ across verifiers.

#### DiMoBench evolution-operator ablations.

We ablate PreCoMem’s three evolution operators on DiMoBench: PROFILE
(hypothesis writing), CORRECT (surprisal-gated soft correction), and
MAINTAIN (reinforcement/smoothing). We report accuracy every 10 turns
from turn 150 to 350. As shown in
[Figure 4](#S3.F4 "In 3.3 Ablation studies and belief-drift adaptation ‣ 3 Experiments ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")(c),
removing PROFILE lowers the final accuracy ceiling (72%), removing
CORRECT substantially slows post-drift recovery, and removing MAINTAIN
leads to high-variance dynamics that do not stabilize within our
evaluation horizon (ending at 54% accuracy).

#### Operator-level analysis.

DiMoBench is designed to separate three regimes that stress different
update behaviors: a stable pre-change phase (turns $`\approx`$150–200),
a short change-point phase with mixed/ambiguous cues and decoy retrieval
(turns $`\approx`$200–240), and a stable post-change phase where the
latent attitude has reversed (turns $`\approx`$240–350). Under this
structure, the three operators contribute in complementary ways.

PROFILE: buffered adaptation under implicit drift. Many change-point
cues are neither clean confirmations nor decisive contradictions: they
are novel, partially ambiguous, and often entangled with confounders.
PROFILE converts these medium-$`S_{\mathrm{eff}}`$ signals into
low-confidence, testable hypotheses, enabling the system to accumulate
multiple weak cues before committing. This “waiting room” is especially
important on DiMoBench because questions explicitly probe
trend/change-point reasoning and hypothesis-vs-fact distinction. When
PROFILE is removed, the system loses this intermediate representation
and commits weak cues without a waiting room (premature commitment under
decoys), which reduces the asymptotic ceiling (72% vs. 84% for full
PreCoMem).

CORRECT: targeted suppression of outdated beliefs. After the drift,
previously correct high-confidence beliefs become systematically
misleading, and decoy retrieval makes stale evidence likely to reappear.
CORRECT provides a selective revision mechanism by downweighting
contradicted items via the $`\zeta_{t}(m)`$-modulated soft decay while
leaving non-contradictory items largely unchanged. This targeted decay
is crucial for reducing old-memory pull and preventing interference from
pre-change beliefs. Without CORRECT, outdated facts remain competitive
in retrieval, so the agent must rely on accumulating new nodes and
indirect effects to “outvote” entrenched beliefs, yielding a
substantially slower recovery (reaching 75% only around turn 320 vs.
turn 250) and a lower final plateau (77% vs. 84%).

MAINTAIN: consolidation for stability. MAINTAIN acts as a consolidation
step that repeatedly reinforces supported beliefs, which sharpens
confidence-weighted retrieval, increases retrieval reliability
$`\rho_{t}`$ (by making the score mass more concentrated), and
stabilizes the routing signal $`x_{t}(=S_{\mathrm{eff}})`$. It also
underpins the hypothesis lifecycle: hypotheses created by PROFILE
require repeated reinforcement to cross the promotion threshold and
become stable facts. This stabilization is critical in the post-change
phase, where the system should stop oscillating and settle into the
post-change regime. When MAINTAIN is ablated, supported post-change
beliefs cannot be progressively consolidated; retrieval remains diffuse
and volatile, the reliability-adjusted loss becomes noisy, and updates
keep “chasing” confounded evidence. Empirically, this yields persistent
volatility: accuracy continues to fluctuate and ends at 54% at the end
of our evaluation.

#### Route-matched and factorial controls.

Setting the gate score to zero (ES in
[Figure 4](#S3.F4 "In 3.3 Ablation studies and belief-drift adaptation ‣ 3 Experiments ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")(a))
routes every turn to MAINTAIN, so it measures a reinforce-only lower
bound; likewise CW sets $`w\equiv 1`$ globally, so its drop is the total
effect of confidence weighting across retrieval and the whole lifecycle
(every hypothesis then trivially satisfies $`w\geq 0.80`$ and is
promoted at its first lifecycle step, pruning never fires, and soft
decay is overwritten).
[Table 12](#A3.T12 "In Route-matched and factorial controls. ‣ C.4 Component, operator, and control ablations ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
isolates the marginal value of the gate and the two roles of confidence
with three controls on LoCoMo and LongMemEval-S (gpt-4o-mini, three
seeds). (i) A *single-threshold* gate without the PROFILE band, which
sends the medium band to CORRECT, reaches 69.92 / 81.16 ($`-3.65`$ /
$`-3.12`$ against Full); this is the marginal value of the waiting room.
(ii) *Frequency-matched random routing* keeps the operator budget at
Full’s empirical routing frequencies (M/P/C $`=56.2/27.4/16.4\%`$) and
leaves the read path unchanged, but assigns operators at random; it
loses $`11.74`$ / $`11.34`$ with the update frequency held fixed, so the
gain comes from the evidence-to-operator correspondence itself. (iii) A
$`2\times 2`$ factorial over read-time weighting ($`w`$ in
[Equation 1](#S2.E1 "In 2.3 Memory leveraging: evidence recall and response generation ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents"))
and lifecycle confidence ($`w`$ in the operator updates and in the
promotion and pruning tests) shows that removing read-time weighting
alone costs $`1.77`$ / $`0.72`$ points, removing lifecycle confidence
alone costs $`20.69`$ / $`22.63`$, and removing both (the CW variant)
costs $`30.15`$ / $`34.63`$; in half-coded effect terms the main effects
are $`+5.62`$ / $`+6.36`$ for read-time weighting and $`+24.54`$ /
$`+28.27`$ for lifecycle confidence, with an interaction of $`-3.85`$ /
$`-5.64`$, i.e., each factor is worth more when the other is absent.

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
| Variant | LoCoMo | LongMemEval-S |
| Full PreCoMem | 73.57 $`\pm`$ 0.28 | 84.28 $`\pm`$ 0.05 |
| Single-threshold gate (no PROFILE band; medium band $`\to`$ CORRECT) | 69.92 $`\pm`$ 0.29 | 81.16 $`\pm`$ 0.12 |
| Frequency-matched random routing (M/P/C $`=56.2/27.4/16.4\%`$) | 61.83 $`\pm`$ 1.25 | 72.94 $`\pm`$ 1.08 |
| Read-time weighting off, lifecycle confidence on | 71.80 $`\pm`$ 0.43 | 83.56 $`\pm`$ 0.23 |
| Lifecycle confidence off, read-time weighting on | 52.88 $`\pm`$ 0.69 | 61.65 $`\pm`$ 0.68 |
| Both off ($`=`$ w/o CW) | 43.42 $`\pm`$ 0.94 | 49.65 $`\pm`$ 0.91 |

Table 12: Route-matched and factorial controls (LLM-judge accuracy,
gpt-4o-mini, mean $`\pm`$ std over three seeds).

#### LLM-direct-write baseline.

A natural alternative to the hybrid design of
[Section 2.1](#S2.SS1 "2.1 Framework overview ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
is to let the same LLM decide the whole write step. The direct-write
baseline matches PreCoMem in every other component (retrieval,
embedding, Top-$`K{=}10`$, context, judge, seeds, gpt-4.1); the only
change is that the LLM emits the entire write decision as one object,
{route, target_ids, operation, new_weights, new_belief}, whose weights
are written verbatim, replacing the gate, the closed-form updates and
the $`[0,1]`$ clamping.
[Table 13](#A3.T13 "In LLM-direct-write baseline. ‣ C.4 Component, operator, and control ablations ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
shows that accuracy is lower in every seed ($`-2.75`$ on LoCoMo,
$`-3.15`$ on LongMemEval-S) while per-query tokens are slightly higher
(3.2k / 3.9k against 3.1k / 3.8k), so the loss is not offset by
efficiency. The more telling difference is stability: the cross-seed
standard deviation is $`20\times`$ higher on LoCoMo (1.42 against 0.07)
and $`2.1\times`$ higher on LongMemEval-S, because unconstrained numeric
decisions make the memory trajectory run-dependent. Replaying the write
decisions on a frozen store, direct write emits a missing target id in
3.6% of decisions, a self-contradictory operation in 2.7% and invalid
JSON in 1.8% (mutually exclusive categories); such malformed writes
corrupt the store silently and compound, whereas the closed-form updates
stay in $`[0,1]`$ by construction. The loss is spread over all LoCoMo
splits (Single-Hop $`-1.65`$, Multi-Hop $`-4.80`$, Temporal $`-2.70`$,
Open-Domain $`-6.50`$). The deterministic layer is what keeps
long-horizon memory bounded.

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  | LoCoMo | LongMemEval-S |
| PreCoMem (bounded scalars $`+`$ closed-form updates) | 85.06 $`\pm`$ 0.07 | 89.39 $`\pm`$ 0.76 |
| LLM direct write | 82.31 $`\pm`$ 1.42 | 86.24 $`\pm`$ 1.63 |
| Paired difference | $`+2.75`$ | $`+3.15`$ |

Table 13: LLM-direct-write baseline (Overall LLM-judge accuracy,
gpt-4.1, mean $`\pm`$ std over three seeds; the paired difference is the
mean over seeds of PreCoMem minus direct write).

#### Turn-level versus per-item routing.

PreCoMem computes one gate score per turn and routes the whole retrieved
set to a single operator; the per-item magnitudes $`\eta_{t}(m)`$ and
$`\zeta_{t}(m)`$ apply after routing. A turn can therefore reinforce
several beliefs, or correct several beliefs, at once, but it cannot
reinforce one belief, correct another and profile a third in the same
step. To measure how often this matters we annotated gold operators per
retrieved item on 9,006 turns (all LoCoMo dialogues plus the
LongMemEval-S evidence sessions): 3,475 turns touch at least one
retrieved belief, and 95.83% of them are expressible by a single route,
either a single belief or several beliefs sharing one operator. The
remaining 145 turns (1.61% of all turns, 21.4% of the 678 multi-item
turns) are genuinely mixed. A per-item variant, identical in retrieval,
backbone and closed-form updates but routing each item independently,
raises the whole-turn agreement with the gold operators on these 145
turns by $`+5.5`$ points and their question accuracy by $`+3.5`$ points,
at $`+31\%`$ write calls and $`+14\%`$ write tokens, for a net gain of
$`+0.3`$ points over the full evaluation. Given the small net benefit at
a materially higher cost, we keep turn-level routing as the default and
regard per-item claim extraction as a direction for future work.

### C.5 Hyperparameter sensitivity and adaptive variants

#### Sensitivity to retrieval budget $`k`$.

We vary the number of retrieved memory items $`k`$ on LoCoMo.
[Table 14](#A3.T14 "In Sensitivity to retrieval budget 𝑘. ‣ C.5 Hyperparameter sensitivity and adaptive variants ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
shows a clear “fast gain, slow gain, mild drop” trend: Overall accuracy
improves substantially from $`k{=}1`$ to $`k{=}5`$
(75.2$`\rightarrow`$82.3), increases marginally from $`k{=}5`$ to
$`k{=}10`$ (82.3$`\rightarrow`$85.06), and then declines at larger
budgets (85.06$`\rightarrow`$82.5 at $`k{=}20`$). Category-wise,
Temporal is the most sensitive to oversized budgets (84.7 at $`k{=}10`$
vs. 80.3 at $`k{=}20`$).
[Figure 7](#A3.F7 "In Sensitivity to retrieval budget 𝑘. ‣ C.5 Hyperparameter sensitivity and adaptive variants ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
visualizes the same sweep across all four LoCoMo splits with a
broken-$`y`$ axis to keep the high- and low-accuracy bands legible
side-by-side.

Figure 7: Top-$`K`$ sensitivity on LoCoMo (gpt-4.1 backbone). Per-split
accuracy as $`k`$ varies in $`\{1,\ldots,20\}`$. The dashed vertical
line marks our default $`k{=}10`$.

| $`k`$ | Single-Hop | Multi-Hop | Temporal | Open-Domain | Overall |
|-------|------------|-----------|----------|-------------|---------|
| 1     | 80.5       | 71.2      | 72.4     | 39.6        | 75.2    |
| 5     | 88.7       | 78.6      | 80.2     | 45.5        | 82.3    |
| 10    | 90.41      | 83.48     | 84.74    | 48.23       | 85.06   |
````
