````math
Table 14: Accuracy (%) at different retrieval sizes $`k`$ on LoCoMo
(gpt-4.1 backbone). Best results are in bold.

#### Mechanism-level analysis.

We provide a more formal explanation of why oversized $`k`$ can hurt
PreCoMem beyond generic retrieval noise. Recall that PreCoMem uses
entropy-based retrieval reliability $`\rho_{t}`$ and the
max-contradiction term $`d_{\mathrm{conf}}`$:

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\hat{s}_{i}`$ | $`\displaystyle=\frac{s_{i}+\epsilon}{\sum_{j=1}^{k}(s_{j}+\epsilon)},`$ |  | (29) |
|  | $`\displaystyle H_{t}`$ | $`\displaystyle=\frac{-\sum_{i=1}^{k}\hat{s}_{i}\log\hat{s}_{i}}{\log k},`$ |  |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle\rho_{t}`$ | $`\displaystyle=1-H_{t}.`$ |  |  |

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle S_{\mathrm{eff}}(u_{t})=x_{t}`$ | $`\displaystyle=\rho_{t}\Big(\lambda\,d_{\mathrm{sem}}(u_{t},\mathcal{C}_{t})+(1-\lambda)\,d_{\mathrm{conf}}(u_{t},\mathcal{C}_{t})\Big),`$ |  | (30) |
|  | $`\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{conf}}`$ | $`\displaystyle=\max_{m\in\mathcal{C}_{t}}\pi_{t}(m).`$ |  |  |

#### (1) $`k\uparrow`$ flattens scores $`\Rightarrow\rho_{t}\downarrow`$ and CORRECT is suppressed.

The reliability $`\rho_{t}`$ decreases when the normalized score
distribution $`\hat{\mathbf{s}}`$ becomes less concentrated. To make
this explicit, consider a simple extension model where the Top-$`k`$
distribution over the original $`k_{0}`$ items is
$`\mathbf{p}\in\Delta^{k_{0}-1}`$, and increasing $`k`$ adds $`k_{1}`$
additional tail items whose total normalized mass is $`\delta\in(0,1)`$
and is approximately uniform within the tail:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbf{p}^{\prime}=\big((1-\delta)p_{1},\ldots,(1-\delta)p_{k_{0}},\underbrace{\tfrac{\delta}{k_{1}},\ldots,\tfrac{\delta}{k_{1}}}_{k_{1}\ \text{items}}\big),k=k_{0}+k_{1}.
``` |  | (31) |

By concavity of Shannon entropy, injecting nonzero tail mass strictly
increases entropy unless $`\delta=0`$. A direct decomposition yields

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{p}^{\prime})=(1-\delta)\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{p})+h(\delta)+\delta\log k_{1},
``` |  | (32) |

where $`\mathcal{H}(\cdot)`$ is unnormalized entropy and
$`h(\delta)=-\delta\log\delta-(1-\delta)\log(1-\delta)`$ is the binary
entropy. Thus, for typical retrieval where the tail receives
non-negligible probability mass, $`H_{t}`$ increases with $`k`$ and
$`\rho_{t}`$ decreases.

This interacts with the fixed-threshold gate: CORRECT is triggered when
$`x_{t}=S_{\mathrm{eff}}(u_{t})>\theta_{\mathrm{high}}`$. Since
$`x_{t}=\rho_{t}S_{\mathrm{raw}}(u_{t})`$ with
$`S_{\mathrm{raw}}(u_{t})\in[0,1]`$, the event can be rewritten as

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\rho_{t}S_{\mathrm{raw}}(u_{t})>\theta_{\mathrm{high}}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad S_{\mathrm{raw}}(u_{t})>\frac{\theta_{\mathrm{high}}}{\rho_{t}}.
``` |  | (33) |

Therefore, as $`\rho_{t}`$ decreases, the required raw-surprisal level
$`\theta_{\mathrm{high}}/\rho_{t}`$ increases, making reliable
correction harder to trigger and strengthening old-memory pull. This
effect is most visible for Temporal queries, where Top-$`k`$ often mixes
pre- and post-change evidence, yielding a less concentrated score mass
(higher $`H_{t}`$) even when the underlying contradiction is real.

#### (2) $`k\uparrow`$ amplifies spurious contradictions via a max statistic.

The contradiction term uses an order statistic
$`d_{\mathrm{conf}}=\max_{m\in\mathcal{C}_{t}}\pi_{t}(m)`$. If we view
$`\pi_{t}(m)`$ for *irrelevant/mismatched* retrieved items as random
variables with CDF $`F_{0}`$ on $`[0,1]`$, then for $`k`$ candidates,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\Pr\!\left(d_{\mathrm{conf}}>\tau\right)=1-\Pr\!\left(\pi_{t}(m)\leq\tau\right)^{k}=1-F_{0}(\tau)^{k},
``` |  | (34) |

which is monotonically increasing in $`k`$ for any $`\tau\in(0,1)`$ with
$`F_{0}(\tau)\in(0,1)`$. When $`1-F_{0}(\tau)`$ is small, the increase
is approximately linear:

|     |                                              |     |      |
|-----|----------------------------------------------|-----|------|
|     |
       ``` math
       1-F_{0}(\tau)^{k}\approx k\,(1-F_{0}(\tau)).
       ```                                           |     | (35) |

Thus, larger evidence sets increase the probability of observing an
artificially high contradiction score from at least one mismatched
memory, which can (i) inflate $`S_{\mathrm{raw}}`$ through the
$`(1-\lambda)d_{\mathrm{conf}}`$ component and (ii) distract response
generation by presenting a “hard contradiction” that is not actually
relevant to the query. Together with (1), this explains why performance
can decline for large $`k`$ despite higher coverage.

#### Implication.

The empirical peak at $`k{=}10`$ reflects a regime where coverage is
sufficient to retrieve the right evidence, while $`\rho_{t}`$ remains
high enough to permit timely CORRECT actions and the max-contradiction
term remains less exposed to spurious outliers. This motivates using
$`k{=}10`$ as a robust default under a moderate compute budget.

#### Sensitivity to gating thresholds.

Theoretically, one may dynamically compute or adapt the thresholds
(e.g., by estimating the action costs/effects in
[Equation 27](#A2.E27 "In Two-threshold gate. ‣ B.3 Operator and gate derivations ‣ Appendix B Additional method details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")),
which could yield better per-sample or per-stage performance. However,
such on-policy adaptation introduces additional computation and
confounds controlled evaluation because the observed behavior becomes
entangled with an evolving threshold schedule.

We therefore adopt a *fixed* threshold pair
$`(\theta_{\mathrm{low}},\theta_{\mathrm{high}})`$ in all experiments
and justify a reasonable choice via a sensitivity study. For
controllable and cost-bounded ablation, we select a representative
LoCoMo sample (Sample 1; 400 dialogue turns and 105 QA pairs) whose
question types are relatively balanced and whose scale supports rapid
iteration. We sweep
$`\theta_{\mathrm{low}}\in\{0.10,0.15,0.20,0.25,0.30\}`$ and
$`\theta_{\mathrm{high}}\in\{0.50,0.55,0.60,0.65,0.70\}`$ in steps of
$`0.05`$ ($`5{\times}5=25`$ configurations) and evaluate LLM-judge
accuracy on this sample. As shown in
[Figure 8](#A3.F8 "In Sensitivity to gating thresholds. ‣ C.5 Hyperparameter sensitivity and adaptive variants ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents"),
the deployed default $`(0.20,0.60)`$ (red box) sits at the center of a
high-performing plateau: its four direct neighbors $`(0.15,0.60)`$,
$`(0.25,0.60)`$, $`(0.20,0.55)`$ and $`(0.20,0.65)`$ all stay within a
narrow $`1.3`$–$`2.6`$-point band of the peak. Accuracy decays smoothly
toward the corners (down to roughly $`64`$–$`67`$% near
$`\theta_{\mathrm{low}}{=}0.30,\theta_{\mathrm{high}}{=}0.70`$ and the
symmetric corner), confirming that the gate is locally robust rather
than knife-edged.

Importantly, this fixed choice is a trade-off: we do not claim
$`(0.2,0.6)`$ is universally optimal for every LoCoMo sample. Rather, it
provides a stable and interpretable operating point with competitive
accuracy under a fixed computational budget, and we keep it constant
across experiments for fair comparison and reproducible observations.

![Refer to caption](2610.29467v1/theta_accuracy_heatmap.png)

Figure 8: Gating threshold sensitivity. LoCoMo accuracy (Sample 1) over
a $`5{\times}5`$ grid of
$`(\theta_{\mathrm{low}},\theta_{\mathrm{high}})`$ with step $`0.05`$
(25 configurations in total). The red box marks the deployed default
$`(0.20,0.60)`$, which sits at the center of a high-performing plateau;
the four immediate neighbors of the peak stay within $`1.3`$–$`2.6`$
accuracy points, while accuracy degrades smoothly toward the grid
corners.

#### Adaptive thresholding for personalization.

One natural question is whether the fixed gating thresholds
$`(\theta_{\mathrm{low}},\theta_{\mathrm{high}})=(0.2,0.6)`$ are
appropriate for all users, or whether different user populations would
benefit from different thresholds. Intuitively, users differ in *belief
stability*: some keep stable preferences so that short-term deviations
are noise, while others change frequently so that the same deviations
reflect real drift. A fixed threshold cannot simultaneously satisfy both
regimes. We therefore discuss and evaluate a simple
adaptive-thresholding extension of PreCoMem.

#### User stability index.

We summarize each user’s recent dynamics with a scalar *stability index*
$`\psi_{t}\in[0,1]`$, computed from interaction statistics over a
sliding window of length $`W`$:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\psi_{t}=\omega_{1}\,\psi_{\mathrm{corr}}+\omega_{2}\,\psi_{\mathrm{life}}+\omega_{3}\,\psi_{\mathrm{var}},
``` |  | (36) |

where (i)
$`\psi_{\mathrm{corr}}=1-N_{\mathrm{correct}}^{(W)}/N_{\mathrm{total}}^{(W)}`$
captures how rarely CORRECT fires in the recent window, (ii)
$`\psi_{\mathrm{life}}=\min\!\left(1,\bar{L}_{\mathrm{belief}}/L_{\mathrm{ref}}\right)`$
captures average belief lifespan relative to a reference
$`L_{\mathrm{ref}}`$, and (iii)
$`\psi_{\mathrm{var}}=\exp\!\left(-\kappa\cdot\mathrm{Var}\!\left[S_{\mathrm{eff}}^{(W)}\right]\right)`$
captures the variance of effective surprisal. The coefficients satisfy
$`\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{3}=1`$, and we use
$`\omega_{1}{=}0.5,\omega_{2}{=}0.3,\omega_{3}{=}0.2`$ in the
experiments below ($`\kappa`$ and $`\omega_{i}`$ are specific to this
variant and unrelated to the $`\gamma`$ and $`\alpha_{j}`$ of the main
text).

#### Threshold adaptation.

We then map $`\psi_{t}`$ to thresholds by linear interpolation between a
conservative upper configuration and an aggressive lower configuration:

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\theta_{\mathrm{low}}(t)`$ | $`\displaystyle=\theta_{\mathrm{low}}^{\min}+\psi_{t}\!\left(\theta_{\mathrm{low}}^{\max}-\theta_{\mathrm{low}}^{\min}\right),`$ |  | (37) |
|  | $`\displaystyle\theta_{\mathrm{high}}(t)`$ | $`\displaystyle=\theta_{\mathrm{high}}^{\min}+\psi_{t}\!\left(\theta_{\mathrm{high}}^{\max}-\theta_{\mathrm{high}}^{\min}\right),`$ |  |  |

with $`\theta_{\mathrm{low}}\!\in\![0.10,0.35]`$ and
$`\theta_{\mathrm{high}}\!\in\![0.40,0.80]`$. Intuitively, a stable user
($`\psi_{t}\!\to\!1`$) receives larger thresholds, so the system
requires stronger evidence before updating; a dynamic user
($`\psi_{t}\!\to\!0`$) receives smaller thresholds, so the system reacts
faster. To avoid oscillation, we smooth the thresholds with an
exponential moving average with coefficient $`\mu{=}0.1`$.

#### Theoretical view.

From a Bayesian perspective, the thresholds encode a prior on belief
persistence: stable users have a stronger prior that beliefs are
unchanged, so a higher $`\theta_{\mathrm{high}}`$ corresponds to
requiring stronger likelihood evidence to revise them, and vice versa
for dynamic users. From a signal detection perspective,
$`\theta_{\mathrm{low}}`$ and $`\theta_{\mathrm{high}}`$ trade off
false-update rate (triggering CORRECT on noise) against missed-update
rate (failing to trigger on genuine drift); since these two error rates
have different relative costs for different users, the Bayes-optimal
decision boundary is user-dependent.
[Equation 37](#A3.E37 "In Threshold adaptation. ‣ C.5 Hyperparameter sensitivity and adaptive variants ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
is a minimal realization of this idea that only reads summary statistics
PreCoMem already maintains and introduces no extra trainable parameters.

#### Empirical results on LoCoMo.

We evaluate the adaptive variant, denoted PreCoMem (DT), on the full
LoCoMo benchmark with two backbones (gpt-4o-mini and gpt-4.1), keeping
every other setting identical to the main experiments. As shown in
[Table 15](#A3.T15 "In Empirical results on LoCoMo. ‣ C.5 Hyperparameter sensitivity and adaptive variants ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents"),
relaxing the fixed thresholds to
[Equation 37](#A3.E37 "In Threshold adaptation. ‣ C.5 Hyperparameter sensitivity and adaptive variants ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
yields consistent gains in overall accuracy ($`+0.98`$ on gpt-4o-mini,
$`+1.15`$ on gpt-4.1), with the largest improvement on the *Open-Domain*
subset under gpt-4o-mini ($`+3.92`$), which is the category most exposed
to long-horizon drift. The same trend holds on LongMemEval-S, where
PreCoMem (DT) lifts the overall score from $`84.28`$ to $`85.36`$ under
gpt-4o-mini, with the largest gains on KU (knowledge update, $`+1.62`$)
and SSP (single-session preference, $`+1.95`$); these are precisely the
question types that require per-user calibration of how aggressively to
update beliefs.

| Backbone    | Method           | Single | Multi | Temp. | Open  | Avg.  |
|-------------|------------------|--------|-------|-------|-------|-------|
| gpt-4o-mini | PreCoMem (fixed) | 81.41  | 68.49 | 66.21 | 39.63 | 73.57 |
|             | PreCoMem (DT)    | 82.27  | 69.31 | 67.04 | 43.55 | 74.55 |
| gpt-4.1     | PreCoMem (fixed) | 90.41  | 83.48 | 84.74 | 48.23 | 85.06 |
|             | PreCoMem (DT)    | 91.42  | 85.16 | 85.81 | 47.78 | 86.21 |

Table 15: Adaptive vs. fixed thresholds on LoCoMo (accuracy %,
gpt-4o-mini and gpt-4.1). PreCoMem (DT) personalizes
$`(\theta_{\mathrm{low}},\theta_{\mathrm{high}})`$ via the user
stability index in
[Equations 36](#A3.E36 "In User stability index. ‣ C.5 Hyperparameter sensitivity and adaptive variants ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
and [37](#A3.E37 "Equation 37 ‣ Threshold adaptation. ‣ C.5 Hyperparameter sensitivity and adaptive variants ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents");
PreCoMem (fixed) uses $`(0.2,0.6)`$. DT improves the overall average on
both backbones, with the largest gain on the drift-heavy *Open* category
under gpt-4o-mini, without any retraining.

These results show that adaptive thresholding is an almost-free
extension that further personalizes PreCoMem for users with different
stability profiles. We keep the fixed-threshold setting as the default
in the main paper for controllable comparison, but we consider adaptive
thresholding a natural deployment-time configuration, especially in
long-horizon and cross-user settings.

### C.6 DiMoBench: construction and extended-horizon stress test

#### Construction pipeline and characteristics.

DiMoBench (Dialogue Implicit Memory Drift Benchmark) is designed to
isolate *belief-drift adaptation* under sparse and implicit cues. The
released suite consists of *five* 350-turn two-speaker dialogues, each
built around a distinct persona drawn from a different combination of
Big-Five extremes and life stages; for clarity the exposition below
focuses on one canonical persona, but every quantitative result on
DiMoBench (the dynamics in
[Figure 4](#S3.F4 "In 3.3 Ablation studies and belief-drift adaptation ‣ 3 Experiments ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")(b)
and the operator ablation in
[Figure 4](#S3.F4 "In 3.3 Ablation studies and belief-drift adaptation ‣ 3 Experiments ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")(c))
is averaged across the five personas to avoid a single-persona artifact;
the human–judge agreement audit in
[Table 8](#A3.T8 "In LLM-as-a-judge validation across benchmarks. ‣ C.3 Extended results and judge validation ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
uses the canonical persona, whose per-type accuracies lie within
$`0.15`$ points of the five-persona means. Within each persona the
user’s latent attitude toward a target topic remains stable in turns
1–200, gradually reverses in turns 201–210 via 3–4 implicit events, and
is then reinforced with denser behavioral signals in turns 211–240
before entering a low-frequency maintenance stage in turns 241–350.

To stress long-horizon robustness, DiMoBench injects confounders: every
30 turns we insert irrelevant chit-chat or hypothetical narration that
shares surface keywords with the target topic, and we introduce multiple
similar entity names to induce attribution ambiguity. Each persona
contributes 200 QA pairs distributed across the dialogue (denser
sampling near the change point and sparser in the long maintenance tail;
$`1{,}000`$ in total across the five personas), covering (i)
current-state inference, (ii) history recall, (iii) change-point
detection, and (iv) hypothesis vs. fact distinction. Importantly,
questions avoid explicit sentiment keywords and instead require
reasoning from behavioral cues (plans, actions, reactions), making drift
detection and correction rely on implicit evidence chains rather than
lexical matches.

#### Why DiMoBench is needed.

Existing long-horizon dialogue benchmarks predominantly evaluate the
*read path*: whether a system can correctly retrieve and reason over a
static conversational record (LoCoMo \[[28](#bib.bib33)\]) or whether it
can infer implicit personalization from accumulated preferences
(PersonaMem-v2 \[[16](#bib.bib37)\]). None of them explicitly
stress-test the *write path*, i.e., whether a memory system can
correctly decide *when to maintain, update, or correct* its stored
beliefs under gradual, implicit drift. DiMoBench is designed to fill
this gap along three axes that are absent from existing benchmarks
simultaneously: (i) *continuous non-stationarity*, where the latent
attitude reverses gradually over hundreds of turns without any explicit
announcement; (ii) *implicit behavioral cues*, where the drift manifests
only through activity choices, reactions to invitations, and hedging
language rather than sentiment keywords; and (iii) *adversarial
retrieval interference*, where five tagged interference types
(entity_confusable, keyword_trap, temporal_decoy, hypothetical_vs_fact,
none) systematically probe two stability–plasticity failure modes:
*old-memory pull* and *premature correction*. This write-path
orientation is what lets DiMoBench reveal the distinct contributions of
MAINTAIN, PROFILE, and CORRECT in
[Section C.4](#A3.SS4.SSS0.Px3 "DiMoBench evolution-operator ablations. ‣ C.4 Component, operator, and control ablations ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents"),
which cannot be isolated on LoCoMo or PersonaMem-v2 alone.

#### Six-stage construction pipeline.

DiMoBench is constructed by a six-stage progressive pipeline with an
explicit *generator–verifier separation* (GPT-4o for generation,
Gemini-2.5-Pro for verification) to avoid self-reinforcing biases.
[Figure 9](#A3.F9 "In Six-stage construction pipeline. ‣ C.6 DiMoBench: construction and extended-horizon stress test ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
visualizes the full pipeline as an end-to-end flow, and
[Table 16](#A3.T16 "In Six-stage construction pipeline. ‣ C.6 DiMoBench: construction and extended-horizon stress test ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
summarizes the stages; details follow below.

![Refer to caption](2610.29467v1/dimobench.png)

Figure 9: DiMoBench six-stage construction pipeline. A generator
(GPT-4o) produces personas, life skeletons, dialogues, and QA pairs; an
independent verifier (Gemini-2.5-Pro) cross-validates each QA pair along
six criteria, and a 20% stratified human audit with
$`\kappa\!\geq\!0.75`$ provides final quality control.

| Stage | Name | Model | Output |
|----|----|----|----|
| 1 | Persona construction | GPT-4o | User profile + core trait slot |
| 2 | Life skeleton & turn | GPT-4o | Event timeline + drift trigger |
| 3 | Dialogue generation | GPT-4o | 35 sessions $`\times`$ 10 turns |
| 4 | QA generation | GPT-4o | 200 QA pairs across 4 categories |
| 5 | Consistency verification | Gemini-2.5-Pro | Cross-validated QA (6 criteria) |
| 6 | Human–machine review | Human + LLM | 20% stratified audit, $`\kappa\!\geq\!0.75`$ |

Table 16: DiMoBench construction pipeline. Generator and verifier are
deliberately different models; a 20% stratified human review provides
final quality control.

*Stage 1 (Persona).* Each persona is characterized by one *core
emotional/behavioral slot* (e.g., “attitude toward social activities”)
with a well-defined initial state and target state, a set of Big-Five
personality dimensions, a small social circle of 2–3 named characters
(some deliberately confusable to enable interference evaluation),
routine daily activities, and a psychologically plausible drift trigger.

*Stage 2 (Life skeleton).* The 350-turn trajectory is organized into 35
sessions of 10 turns each. Sessions 1–20 (turns 1–200) establish
baseline behavior; session 21 (turns 201–210) introduces the drift
trigger via 3–4 implicit events; sessions 22–24 (turns 211–240)
reinforce the new behavior with denser cues; sessions 25–35 (turns
241–350) form a low-frequency maintenance stage with monotonically
reduced divergence from a steady post-drift baseline.

*Stage 3 (Dialogue generation).* Sessions are generated sequentially
under cumulative context management. Generation rules enforce natural
small talk and topic shifts, implicit expression through activity
choices and hedging language, no meta-commentary (the user never says
“I’ve changed”), and frequent temporal markers (“last weekend”, “this
month”) for temporal reasoning.

*Stage 4 (QA generation).* 200 QA pairs span all temporal phases
(pre-change: 90; change-point: 10; post-change: 100). Each QA carries
explicit evidence pointers (D{session}:{turn}), an interference tag, and
a reasoning-type label.

*Stage 5 (Consistency verification).* Each QA is cross-validated by
Gemini-2.5-Pro along six dimensions: (i) *answer grounding* (is the
answer supported by cited evidence?), (ii) *evidence completeness* (are
all relevant turns cited?), (iii) *temporal consistency* (does the
answer reflect the correct phase?), (iv) *entity accuracy*, (v)
*interference validity* (does the tag match the question?), and (vi)
*drift coherence* (is the behavioral change gradual and plausible?). QA
pairs marked REVISE are regenerated by GPT-4o using verifier feedback
(max 3 rounds); pairs marked REJECT are discarded. We target an
$`\geq\!95\%`$ ACCEPT rate on the auto-verification pass.

*Stage 6 (Human–machine review).* Three graduate-level annotators with
NLP research experience underwent a 2-hour calibration session covering
implicit emotional expression, temporal reasoning, and entity
disambiguation. All annotators independently label the same 20 QA pairs,
and independent annotation proceeds only after inter-annotator agreement
reaches $`\kappa\!\geq\!0.75`$ (Cohen’s $`\kappa`$). A 20% stratified
sample (stratified by temporal phase, interference type, and QA
category) receives full human review; in addition, one annotator per
persona performs a full 350-turn dialogue-coherence assessment. Quality
targets are answer correctness $`\geq\!95\%`$, dialogue naturalness
$`\geq\!8.0/10`$, and drift subtlety $`\geq\!7.5/10`$.

#### Representative QA examples.

To make DiMoBench concrete, we include three canonical QA pairs
illustrating each temporal phase and several interference tags:

Additional traces that walk through the operator decisions on DiMoBench,
including the retrieved Top-$`K`$ evidence and the resulting memory
edits, are provided in
[Section C.11](#A3.SS11.SSS0.Px4 "End-to-end trace examples on DiMoBench. ‣ C.11 System inspectability, traces, and qualitative analyses ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents").
All five 350-turn dialogues, the per-persona QA pairs, generation
prompts, verification prompts, and human-review scripts are released
with the paper at
[https://github.com/ZhongWishing/PreCoMem](https://github.com/ZhongWishing/PreCoMem).

#### DiMoBench-XL construction.

To stress-test the system beyond DiMoBench’s 350-turn horizon and across
more personas, we constructed DiMoBench-XL, a suite of *five* 1050-turn
dialogues, each with three sequential implicit drifts. Following the
DiMoBench pipeline in
[Section C.6](#A3.SS6.SSS0.Px1 "Construction pipeline and characteristics. ‣ C.6 DiMoBench: construction and extended-horizon stress test ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents"),
we instantiate five user personas (each with a distinct demographic and
dispositional profile, sampled to span both Big-Five extremes and
different life stages); within each persona we keep five independent
attributes (drink preference, exercise habit, home city, employment
status, social style) and apply three drifts at turns
$`\{280,580,870\}`$, each shifting one or two attributes via
$`20`$–$`30`$ behavioral cues distributed across the surrounding
session. Per persona we sample 20 evaluation QA pairs every 50 turns
($`420`$ pairs each, $`2{,}100`$ in total), balanced across four
reasoning types and five interference tags. The whole suite is verified
by Gemini-2.5-Pro with the same six-criterion protocol as DiMoBench,
achieving a $`96.4\%`$ average ACCEPT rate across personas (range
$`94.8`$–$`97.6\%`$) after one revision round.

#### Trajectory data.

[Table 17](#A3.T17 "In Trajectory data. ‣ C.6 DiMoBench: construction and extended-horizon stress test ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
reports the full $`N_{\mathrm{raw}}`$ / $`N_{\mathrm{eff}}`$ / per-bin
counts every $`100`$ turns and at the three drift points on a
representative DiMoBench-XL persona. $`N_{\mathrm{eff}}`$ enters its
$`25.8`$–$`28.5`$ plateau by turn $`200`$, dips by at most $`1.1`$
points at a drift on this persona (at most $`1.6`$ across the five
personas), and recovers within $`\sim`$50 turns to the same band.
$`N_{\mathrm{active}}`$ (items with $`w_{t}\!\geq\!0.8`$) stabilizes at
$`17`$–$`18`$ across all three drift epochs, indicating that each drift
replaces the previous active set rather than accreting onto it. Across
the five personas the per-bin $`N_{\mathrm{eff}}`$ trajectory stays
within $`\pm 1.4`$ of the values reported here, and
$`N_{\mathrm{active}}`$ never leaves the $`16`$–$`19`$ band after turn
$`200`$, confirming the same qualitative behavior without persona-level
outliers.

| $`t`$ | $`N_{\mathrm{raw}}`$ | $`N_{\mathrm{eff}}`$ | $`N_{\mathrm{active}}`$ | $`N_{\mathrm{stable}}`$ | $`N_{\mathrm{weak}}`$ | $`N_{\mathrm{depr}}`$ | $`N_{\mathrm{eff}}/N_{\mathrm{raw}}`$ |
|----|----|----|----|----|----|----|----|
| 100 | 26 | 21.0 | 13 | 8 | 3 | 2 | 0.81 |
| 200 | 44 | 25.8 | 16 | 11 | 7 | 7 | 0.59 |
| $`280^{\dagger}`$ | 57 | 26.5 | 17 | 11 | 9 | 20 | 0.46 |
| 300 | 60 | 26.7 | 17 | 12 | 9 | 22 | 0.45 |
| 400 | 73 | 27.4 | 17 | 12 | 8 | 36 | 0.38 |
| 500 | 81 | 28.5 | 18 | 13 | 8 | 42 | 0.35 |
| $`580^{\dagger}`$ | 89 | 27.4 | 17 | 11 | 10 | 51 | 0.31 |
| 600 | 92 | 27.6 | 17 | 12 | 9 | 54 | 0.30 |
| 700 | 103 | 28.0 | 18 | 12 | 9 | 64 | 0.27 |
| 800 | 113 | 27.9 | 18 | 12 | 8 | 75 | 0.25 |
| $`870^{\dagger}`$ | 121 | 27.6 | 17 | 11 | 9 | 84 | 0.23 |
| 900 | 123 | 28.2 | 18 | 12 | 8 | 85 | 0.23 |
| 1000 | 128 | 28.0 | 18 | 12 | 8 | 90 | 0.22 |
| 1050 | 131 | 28.2 | 18 | 12 | 8 | 93 | 0.22 |

Table 17: DiMoBench-XL memory trajectory (representative persona). Every
$`100`$ turns (and at the three drift points) on one of the five
DiMoBench-XL personas; per-persona deviations from this trajectory stay
within $`\pm 1.4`$ on $`N_{\mathrm{eff}}`$ throughout the run. The four
count columns partition $`\mathcal{B}_{t}`$ by confidence:
$`N_{\mathrm{active}}`$ ($`w_{t}\!\geq\!0.8`$), $`N_{\mathrm{stable}}`$
($`0.5\!\leq\!w_{t}\!<\!0.8`$), $`N_{\mathrm{weak}}`$
($`0.1\!\leq\!w_{t}\!<\!0.5`$) and $`N_{\mathrm{depr}}`$
($`w_{t}\!<\!0.1`$); the last column is the ratio
$`N_{\mathrm{eff}}/N_{\mathrm{raw}}`$. Drift turns
($`t\!\in\!\{280,580,870\}`$) are marked with $`\dagger`$.

#### QA accuracy stability.

[Table 18](#A3.T18 "In QA accuracy stability. ‣ C.6 DiMoBench: construction and extended-horizon stress test ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
aggregates QA accuracy across the five DiMoBench-XL personas in five
time windows (means across the five personas). Across $`1050`$ turns and
three drifts, Overall accuracy decays only $`2.3`$ points (from
$`84.3\%`$ to $`82.0\%`$ on average) and the average Top-10 retrieval
confidence stays at or above $`0.78`$ in every persona, confirming that
long-horizon retention is gracefully degraded by confidence weighting
rather than catastrophically collapsed; persona-level standard
deviations stay below $`1.0`$ accuracy point in every window.

| Turn window               | \#QA | Acc. (%) | Avg Top-10 $`w`$ | Contam.@10 (%) |
|---------------------------|------|----------|------------------|----------------|
| $`\phantom{0}50`$–$`250`$ | 80   | 84.3     | 0.82             | 0.4            |
| $`250`$–$`500`$           | 100  | 83.6     | 0.80             | 0.6            |
| $`500`$–$`750`$           | 100  | 83.1     | 0.79             | 0.9            |
| $`750`$–$`1000`$          | 100  | 82.4     | 0.78             | 1.2            |
| $`1000`$–$`1050`$         | 40   | 82.0     | 0.78             | 1.3            |

Table 18: QA accuracy and retrieval quality on DiMoBench-XL across time
windows. Numbers are averaged across the five DiMoBench-XL personas;
per-window standard deviations across personas are $`\leq 1.0`$ accuracy
point and $`\leq 0.02`$ for the Top-10 weight, and are omitted for
readability. Average Top-10 retrieval confidence stays at or above
$`0.78`$ throughout $`1050`$ turns; Overall accuracy decays by only
$`2.3`$ points.

### C.7 Memory dynamics: footprint, contamination, and retention

#### Headline metrics and retrieval contamination.

[Table 19](#A3.T19 "In Headline metrics and retrieval contamination. ‣ C.7 Memory dynamics: footprint, contamination, and retention ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
summarizes the headline scalability numbers used in
[Section 3.5](#S3.SS5 "3.5 Memory scalability ‣ 3 Experiments ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents").
Below we further examine whether deprecated FACTs leak into Top-$`k`$
retrieval.

| Setting | $`N_{\mathrm{raw}}^{\mathrm{end}}`$ | $`N_{\mathrm{eff}}^{\mathrm{end}}`$ | Contam.@10 (%) | QA $`\Delta`$ (%) |
|----|----|----|----|----|
| DiMoBench (350 t.) | 62 | 27.6 | 0.6 | $`-`$0.6 |
| DiMoBench-XL (1050 t.) | 131 | 28.2 | 1.0 | $`-`$2.3 |

Table 19: Memory scalability headline metrics. $`N_{\mathrm{raw}}`$
grows linearly with the conversation, but $`N_{\mathrm{eff}}`$ converges
to a stable plateau and the contamination of deprecated FACTs in Top-10
retrieval stays at or below $`1.3\%`$ in every window even after 1050
turns and three drifts. Both rows average over their five personas;
per-persona deviations are $`\leq 0.5`$ on
$`N_{\mathrm{eff}}^{\mathrm{end}}`$ and $`\leq 0.4`$ on Contam.@10.

A second concern is whether deprecated FACTs ($`w_{t}\!<\!0.1`$) leak
into Top-$`k`$ retrieval and silently bias the response. We post-analyze
*every* evaluation query on DiMoBench and DiMoBench-XL with three
different retrieval budgets $`k\!\in\!\{5,10,25\}`$, recording (i) the
score-mass ratio
$`\sum_{m\in\mathcal{C}_{t},\,w_{t}<0.1}s(u_{t},m)/\sum_{m\in\mathcal{C}_{t}}s(u_{t},m)`$,
(ii) the fraction of Top-$`k`$ slots occupied by deprecated FACTs, and
(iii) the average $`w_{t}`$ of retrieved items. Results in

remains in $`[0.71,\,0.84]`$ across all settings, showing the score is
dominated by high-confidence beliefs.

#### FACT pruning ablation.

The default PreCoMem (config A0) prunes HYPOTHESIS items at
$`w_{t}\!<\!0.1`$ but never deletes FACTs; this is intentional because
deprecated FACTs serve as immutable provenance hooks for the
corresponding episodic-cache traces. To validate that this provenance
choice is not a hidden scalability liability, we test two pruning
variants: A1 also prunes FACTs at $`w_{t}\!<\!0.05`$, and A2 prunes
FACTs at the same $`w_{t}\!<\!0.10`$ used for hypotheses.
[Table 22](#A3.T22 "In FACT pruning ablation. ‣ C.7 Memory dynamics: footprint, contamination, and retention ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
reports performance and final memory size on LoCoMo (gpt-4o-mini,
10-sample mean) and on DiMoBench (350-turn end-state).

|  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | LoCoMo (gpt-4o-mini) |  |  |  |  |  | DiMoBench (350 t. end) |  |  |
| Config | Single | Multi | Temp. | Open | Overall | $`N_{\mathrm{raw}}^{\mathrm{end}}`$ | Acc. (%) | $`N_{\mathrm{raw}}^{\mathrm{end}}`$ | $`N_{\mathrm{eff}}^{\mathrm{end}}`$ |
| A0 (default; no FACT prune) | 81.41 | 68.49 | 66.21 | 39.63 | 73.57 | 287 | 84.0 | 62 | 27.6 |
| A1 (FACT prune $`w\!<\!0.05`$) | 81.36 | 68.42 | 66.18 | 39.55 | 73.51 | 168 | 83.8 | 41 | 27.5 |
| A2 (FACT prune $`w\!<\!0.10`$) | 80.95 | 68.05 | 65.74 | 39.21 | 73.06 | 142 | 82.9 | 33 | 26.7 |

Table 22: FACT pruning ablation. A1 ($`w\!<\!0.05`$) shrinks raw memory
by $`41\%`$ on LoCoMo and $`34\%`$ on DiMoBench while losing
$`\leq\!0.2`$ accuracy points, inside seed variance. A2 ($`w\!<\!0.10`$)
starts pruning items that still have residual influence and degrades
accuracy by $`0.5`$–$`1.1`$ points.

A1 cuts $`N_{\mathrm{raw}}`$ from $`287`$ to $`168`$ on LoCoMo while
losing only $`0.06`$ Overall points (well below the seed standard
deviation of $`0.28`$), confirming that the additional FACTs A0 retains
were already behaviorally inactive. A2 starts pruning items that still
carry residual signal and consequently loses $`0.5`$ Overall on LoCoMo
and $`1.1`$ points on DiMoBench. We therefore default to A0 for
provenance and recommend A1 only for storage-bounded deployments.

#### Cross-system memory footprint.

A more detailed cross-system footprint comparison, including the
per-item-confidence design dimension, is reported in the main paper as
[Table 2](#S3.T2 "In 3.5 Memory scalability ‣ 3 Experiments ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
([Section 3.5](#S3.SS5 "3.5 Memory scalability ‣ 3 Experiments ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")).
In short, on the ten LoCoMo dialogues ($`\bar{N}\!=\!588`$ turns),
PreCoMem-A1 stores $`168`$ items, lower than both O-Mem ($`232`$) and
EverMemOS ($`264`$), while delivering $`+2.6`$ and $`+3.4`$ Overall
points respectively; O-Mem and EverMemOS commit each interaction through
a discrete decision, so their stores admit no per-item confidence and
the effective count $`N_{\mathrm{eff}}`$ is undefined.

### C.8 Retrieval dependence and reliability calibration

The write path acts only on the retrieved set $`\mathcal{C}_{t}`$: if
the belief that should be revised is not retrieved, the contradiction
cannot be detected and CORRECT cannot fire in that turn. This section
quantifies that dependence and then examines what the reliability
coefficient $`\rho_{t}`$ actually tracks.

#### Belief-update recall and failure decomposition.

We call an *update-due event* a point at which a stored belief must be
revised for a later question to be answered correctly (one per
evaluation question on DiMoBench and DiMoBench-XL, one per state
transition with a pre-existing belief on AMemGym, and one per
knowledge-update turn on LongMemEval-S KU), and we measure the Recall@10
of the belief due for update, i.e., how often it enters the write-path
Top-10 at that event.
[Table 23](#A3.T23 "In Belief-update recall and failure decomposition. ‣ C.8 Retrieval dependence and reliability calibration ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
shows that recall covers most events (89–95%), leaving an explicit 5–11%
blind spot, and that misses matter: question accuracy on events whose
target was retrieved exceeds that on missed events by 16–22 points.
Retrieval is favorable here because a contradictory utterance shares the
attribute of the belief it contradicts with opposite polarity, so the
target scores high on the semantic and lexical terms of
[Equation 1](#S2.E1 "In 2.3 Memory leveraging: evidence recall and response generation ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents").
Decomposing all update-due events pooled over the four benchmarks, 89.3%
succeed; the residual 10.7% splits into unrecovered retrieval misses
(5.4%; a further 1.4% of events are missed at retrieval but corrected
later), gate misses (3.1%; the target was retrieved but the turn was not
routed to CORRECT) and localization misses (2.2%; the turn was routed to
CORRECT but the target was not decayed). Retrieval is thus the largest
single source, with half of the failures (5.3 of the 10.7 points)
sitting in gating and localization; on LongMemEval-S KU, where each
update is stated once, retrieval misses dominate. A miss does not stall
adaptation: the observation still enters the episodic cache and can form
a hypothesis, and 20% of missed events (147 of 718) are corrected later
by subsequent cues or hypothesis promotion.

To separate association from causation we force the target belief into
the Top-10 at every missed event (displacing the lowest-ranked item) and
re-run the write path and the question. This closes only 26–32% of the
hit–miss gap (last column of
[Table 23](#A3.T23 "In Belief-update recall and failure decomposition. ‣ C.8 Retrieval dependence and reliability calibration ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")):
forcing the hit raises accuracy on the affected questions by 4.5–6.5
points, while the rest of the gap reflects that missed events are
intrinsically harder and that gating and localization can still fail
once the target is retrieved.

| Benchmark | Recall@10 | QA (hit) | QA (miss) | QA (forced hit) | Gap recovered |
|----|----|----|----|----|----|
| DiMoBench | 95.0 $`\pm`$ 0.4 | 84.8 | 64.2 | 70.7 | 32% |
| DiMoBench-XL | 93.0 $`\pm`$ 0.2 | 84.7 | 63.0 | 69.4 | 29% |
| LongMemEval-S KU | 90.0 $`\pm`$ 0.5 | 93.4 | 75.8 | 80.3 | 26% |
| AMemGym | 89.1 $`\pm`$ 0.5 | 72.1 | 55.8 | 60.6 | 29% |

Table 23: Belief-update recall and the cost of a miss. Recall@10 of the
belief due for update (mean $`\pm`$ std over three seeds); question
accuracy (%) on events whose target was retrieved (hit) or not (miss),
and on missed events after forcing the target into the Top-10 (forced
hit); the last column is the share of the hit–miss gap recovered by the
forced hit. AMemGym accuracies are changed-state probe accuracies.

#### What $`\rho_{t}`$ measures: calibration.

$`\rho_{t}=1-H_{t}`$ is the normalized concentration of the Top-$`K`$
retrieval scores. It does not judge whether an individual retrieved item
is correct; it weights how far the surprisal computed from that recall
can be trusted, and because it enters only multiplicatively it can make
the gate more conservative but never more aggressive. Two cases
illustrate the behavior. If several relevant memories are correctly
retrieved with similar scores, $`\rho_{t}`$ is lower and the update is
dampened and deferred; no information is lost, since supporting evidence
keeps accumulating through the hypothesis lifecycle and a genuine change
is still committed after slightly more confirmation. If a wrong memory
receives a very high score, $`\rho_{t}`$ is high, but $`\rho_{t}`$ is
deliberately not the sole trigger: whether CORRECT fires still depends
on $`d_{\mathrm{conf}}`$, an NLI verifier run against the actual new
utterance, and the change is a bounded, reversible soft decay that keeps
provenance.

To check that concentration is a usable proxy for evidence precision, we
stratify the 2,829 gate events of the AMemGym runs (943 per seed) into
quintiles of $`\rho_{t}`$ and measure, per quintile, the precision of
the retrieved set against the user’s current gold state, the recall of
the belief due for update, the accuracy of the contradiction decision
against 400 blind-annotated events (80 per quintile), the correctness of
the resulting update, the share of CORRECT firings judged unwarranted
(false-CORRECT), and the accuracy of the next state-query probe on the
touched attribute
([Table 24](#A3.T24 "In What 𝜌_𝑡 measures: calibration. ‣ C.8 Retrieval dependence and reliability calibration ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")).
Precision, contradiction accuracy, update correctness and downstream
accuracy all rise monotonically from Q1 to Q5, and false corrections
fall from 2.1% to 0.6% (only 5 of the 612 CORRECT firings were judged
false overall), whereas recall is non-monotone and peaks in the middle
quintiles. $`\rho_{t}`$ therefore tracks evidence precision, as a
precision weight on the prediction error should.

|                                            |      |      |      |      |      |
|--------------------------------------------|------|------|------|------|------|
|                                            | Q1   | Q2   | Q3   | Q4   | Q5   |
| Gold-evidence Precision@$`K`$ $`\uparrow`$ | 0.58 | 0.68 | 0.76 | 0.82 | 0.87 |
| Gold-evidence Recall@$`K`$                 | 0.86 | 0.88 | 0.89 | 0.86 | 0.82 |
| Contradiction accuracy $`\uparrow`$        | 0.71 | 0.79 | 0.84 | 0.89 | 0.92 |
| Update correctness $`\uparrow`$            | 0.74 | 0.80 | 0.85 | 0.90 | 0.93 |
| False-CORRECT (%) $`\downarrow`$           | 2.1  | 1.0  | 0.8  | 0.7  | 0.6  |
| Downstream probe accuracy $`\uparrow`$     | 0.66 | 0.71 | 0.75 | 0.78 | 0.81 |

Table 24: Calibration of $`\rho_{t}`$ on AMemGym (2,829 gate events over
three seeds, split into $`\rho_{t}`$ quintiles from Q1, lowest, to Q5,
highest; means over seeds). Precision@$`K`$ and Recall@$`K`$ use
different references (the user’s current gold state versus the single
belief due for update); false-CORRECT is the share of CORRECT firings in
the quintile judged unwarranted in the blind audit.

#### Correction delay during drift.

A related concern is that genuine drift produces mixed retrieval
evidence, old facts and new contradictions both in the Top-$`K`$, which
raises $`H_{t}`$ and may delay CORRECT precisely when it is needed. We
measure this on the 600 gold transitions of the AMemGym runs (20
profiles $`\times`$ 10 transitions $`\times`$ 3 seeds). In the first
period after a change, a fresh belief node encoding the new state is
created for 91.3% of the transitions and enters the answering Top-10 for
88.7%; the remaining 2.6% is a read-path ranking loss, since the new
belief exists but does not enter the answering Top-10. A low
$`\rho_{t}`$ delays a warranted CORRECT in 14.2% of the transitions; of
these delays 78.0% clear within one period and 94.0% within two, and
only 2.8% persist to the end of the run. End-to-end, this bounded delay
is what separates PreCoMem’s one-period recovery from the two periods of
AWE and the 1.8–3.2 periods of the ablated variants
([Section C.1](#A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1 "AMemGym. ‣ C.1 Third-party benchmarks: AMemGym and LoCoMo-Plus ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents"));
removing the weighting altogether instead costs 16.76 points on LoCoMo
(RR in
[Figure 4](#S3.F4 "In 3.3 Ablation studies and belief-drift adaptation ‣ 3 Experiments ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")(a)).

The same analysis on DiMoBench, which has gold change points, uses the
$`\rho`$-assessability criterion that a gold shift counts only when the
outdated belief is in the Top-$`K`$ and
$`S_{\mathrm{raw}}>\theta_{\mathrm{high}}`$, so that $`\rho_{t}=1`$
would route the turn to CORRECT; the delay $`d`$ is measured in turns
([Table 25](#A3.T25 "In Correction delay during drift. ‣ C.8 Retrieval dependence and reliability calibration ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")).
Delays are the exception and bounded on both tracks: 7.3% of the
assessable shifts on DiMoBench and 14.8% on DiMoBench-XL are delayed,
and 71.4% of the delays clear within one turn and 85.7% within two.
DiMoBench-XL exceeds the base track, consistent with its three drifts
over 1050 turns and denser decoys. Since DiMoBench counts turns whereas
AMemGym counts periods, the two rates agree in magnitude without sharing
a unit, which is why we report them per track.

| Track | Gold shifts | $`\rho`$-assessable | $`d{=}0`$ | $`d{=}1`$ | $`d{=}2`$ | $`d{>}2`$ | Any delay |
|----|----|----|----|----|----|----|----|
| DiMoBench | 45 | 41 | 38 | 3 | 0 | 0 | $`3/41=7.3\%`$ |
| DiMoBench-XL | 135 | 122 | 104 | 12 | 3 | 3 | $`18/122=14.8\%`$ |

Table 25: CORRECT delay at gold change points on DiMoBench and
DiMoBench-XL (counts pooled over three seeds). A shift is
$`\rho`$-assessable when the outdated belief is in the Top-$`K`$ and
$`S_{\mathrm{raw}}>\theta_{\mathrm{high}}`$; $`d`$ is the delay in
turns.

#### Cold start and genuinely new topics.

A genuinely new topic differs from every stored belief, so its
semantic-deviation term $`d_{\mathrm{sem}}`$ is high while
$`d_{\mathrm{conf}}\approx 0`$, and $`S_{\mathrm{raw}}`$ is driven up by
novelty; even when all absolute similarities are small, one loosely
related item normally dominates the Top-$`K`$, so $`\rho_{t}`$ stays
moderate and $`S_{\mathrm{eff}}`$ lands in the PROFILE band. MAINTAIN
would require an unsurprising cue with low $`d_{\mathrm{sem}}`$, which a
new topic is not. In cold-start probes on the AMemGym profiles, a
first-time cue is written on an empty store 96.5% of the time (579 of
600 probes, each starting from a reset store); when the store is
populated and the cue matches little, a usable belief forms within one
period in 84.2% of cases and within two in 92.7% (600 new-topic probes),
while only 5.6% of 180 distractor cues are written as hypotheses. A low
match therefore costs at most a short confirmation delay.
[Section C.11](#A3.SS11.SSS0.Px11 "Case 3: A new topic entering a populated store. ‣ C.11 System inspectability, traces, and qualitative analyses ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
walks through an example.

### C.9 Retrieval and end-to-end latency analysis

#### Experimental setup.

We conduct all latency evaluations on the LoCoMo dataset. To ensure a
rigorous comparison, we standardize the hardware and model environment
across all systems:

- •
  LLM Backbone: gpt-4o-mini serves as the backbone for all generation
  and retrieval operations.
- •
  Embeddings: All embeddings are generated using Qwen3-Embedding-0.6B
  with 1024 dimensions.
- •
  Configuration: Each baseline operates under its official recommended
  hyperparameters.

#### Metrics.

We report P50 and P95 latency for *both* the retrieval stage and the
full end-to-end (E2E) response:

- •
  Retrieval latency measures only the cost of producing the retrieved
  evidence set $`\mathcal{C}_{t}`$ (memory read, scoring, and any
  re-ranking), and isolates the architectural cost of the memory system
  itself.
- •
  End-to-end (E2E) latency measures the full user-visible time from
  receiving a query $`u_{t}`$ to emitting the final answer, including
  retrieval plus generation.
- •
  P50 (Median): The median latency, representing the typical time
  required to process a query.
- •
  P95 (95th Percentile): The threshold within which 95% of queries are
  completed, reflecting performance stability under high load or
  ambiguity.

#### Results and analysis.

[Table 26](#A3.T26 "In Results and analysis. ‣ C.9 Retrieval and end-to-end latency analysis ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
summarizes the comparative performance. PreCoMem achieves a retrieval
P50 / P95 of 1.95s / 3.86s and an E2E P50 / P95 of 2.35s / 4.36s.

- •
  Efficiency via vectorized scoring: PreCoMem achieves a competitive P50
  by leveraging a confidence-modulated hybrid scoring mechanism
  ([Equation 1](#S2.E1 "In 2.3 Memory leveraging: evidence recall and response generation ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")).
  Rather than relying on computationally intensive generative re-ranking
  or iterative graph traversals, our approach efficiently aggregates
  semantic and lexical signals through parallelizable matrix operations.
  This design ensures that the computational overhead remains modest
  relative to pure vector retrieval, even as the complexity of the
  belief store increases.
- •
  Stability via deterministic flow: A critical advantage of PreCoMem is
  its robustness against latency spikes. Unlike agentic architectures
  (e.g., A-Mem) that may trigger unpredictable, recursive reasoning
  loops to resolve ambiguities, PreCoMem employs a deterministic,
  non-recursive retrieval flow. This architectural choice constrains
  both the retrieval tail (P95: 3.86s) and the E2E tail (P95: 4.36s),
  ensuring consistent performance without the extreme delays (E2E P95 up
  to 7.68s for A-Mem) often observed in stochastic agentic systems.
- •
  Retrieval vs. E2E gap: The gap between retrieval and E2E latency is
  dominated by generation, which is largely independent of the memory
  backend. PreCoMem’s E2E overhead over the retrieval stage (0.4s at
  P50, 0.5s at P95) is comparable to other non-agentic baselines,
  confirming that adding PROFILE/CORRECT does not materially inflate
  generation cost.

| Method          | Retrieval |       | End-to-End |       |
|-----------------|-----------|-------|------------|-------|
|                 | P50       | P95   | P50        | P95   |
| MemoryOS        | 1.66s     | 2.21s | –          | –     |
| MemOS           | 1.69s     | 3.44s | 2.04s      | 3.89s |
| A-Mem           | 1.74s     | 7.23s | 2.09s      | 7.68s |
| Mem0            | 2.44s     | 4.29s | 2.74s      | 4.69s |
| PreCoMem (Ours) | 1.95s     | 3.86s | 2.35s      | 4.36s |

Table 26: Retrieval and end-to-end latency comparison. Evaluated on
LoCoMo with gpt-4o-mini. “Retrieval” is the memory read/scoring cost
only; “E2E” is the full query-to-answer time (retrieval + generation).
PreCoMem obtains competitive typical latency and a well-controlled tail
at both stages, without the extreme P95 spikes seen for recursive
agentic baselines.

### C.10 Structural comparison: why PreCoMem outperforms existing memory systems

Beyond the headline accuracy gains in
[Table 1](#S2.T1 "In Trace and topic write-back. ‣ 2.4 Memory evolution: regime-gated belief updating ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents"),
two structural properties of PreCoMem account for the consistent
improvement and have no direct analogue in any of the nine baselines we
compare against. We summarize the contrast along the two axes that most
directly govern long-horizon behavior.

#### Axis 1: Robustness to noise and faithful preservation of user core traits.

Existing systems represent the importance of an incoming observation
*categorically*. GAM \[[46](#bib.bib27)\] writes one compressed memo per
session into an immutable page-store with no per-item weight;
EverMemOS \[[10](#bib.bib25)\] validates each MemCell within a hard time
interval $`[t_{\mathrm{start}},t_{\mathrm{end}}]`$ and treats it as
expired once the interval closes; TiMem \[[21](#bib.bib32)\] encodes
importance through its five-level temporal hierarchy and explicitly
notes the absence of a storage-time forgetting mechanism;
MemBox \[[38](#bib.bib36)\] stores all events inside a sealed topic box
at uniform weight; O-Mem \[[40](#bib.bib15)\] updates persona slots via
an LLM that selects one of three categorical actions
(Add/Ignore/Update); Mem0 \[[2](#bib.bib16)\] commits
ADD/UPDATE/DELETE/NOOP and physically removes contradicted memories on
DELETE; and A-Mem \[[45](#bib.bib17)\] replaces an existing note with an
LLM-rewritten note via Memory Evolution. None of these systems can defer
commitment when evidence is ambiguous: a weak signal is either ignored
or instantly written as a fact. PreCoMem’s continuous confidence weight
$`w_{t}(m)\!\in\![0,1]`$ paired with the HYPOTHESIS waiting room lets
weak cues accumulate evidence across turns and only graduate into FACT
once support concentrates. The same mechanism preserves established
traits: soft-decay correction
([Equation 10](#S2.E10 "In CORRECT (soft correction). ‣ 2.4 Memory evolution: regime-gated belief updating ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents"))
reduces the influence of contradicted facts without erasing them, so a
transient noise burst cannot remove a long-established trait the way a
hard DELETE or overwrite would. This explains the LongMemEval-S gains on
SSP (single-session preference), MS (multi-session aggregation), and KU
(knowledge update) in
[Table 1](#S2.T1 "In Trace and topic write-back. ‣ 2.4 Memory evolution: regime-gated belief updating ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents"),
where ambiguous and conflicting cues dominate the question pool and
categorical commit decisions are exactly the wrong granularity.

#### Axis 2: Retrieval coherence and avoidance of fragmentation.

Each baseline retrieval substrate carries a structural failure mode that
PreCoMem sidesteps. GAM iterates a deep-research loop over its
page-store, whose answer correctness is order- and budget-sensitive;
EverMemOS issues dense+BM25 retrieval over Atomic Facts, fuses by RRF,
and then runs a sufficiency-check loop that can leave cross-shard
inconsistency between MemCells of the same scene; TiMem performs a
two-stage hierarchical recall whose scope depends on a planner
classifier; MemBox returns full topic boxes wholesale with no within-box
reweighting; O-Mem aggregates three parallel channels (working /
episodic / persona) by union, so irrelevant attribute hits dilute the
context window; Mem0 falls back on a single dense-similarity threshold
and discards deleted-but-relevant facts; and A-Mem performs pure cosine
top-$`k`$ over linked atomic notes, where the fine granularity amplifies
fragmentation noise. All of these substrates therefore require an
explicit reranker, sufficiency loop, or planner to suppress incoherent
reads. PreCoMem instead multiplies a tri-signal relevance score by the
per-item confidence $`w_{t}(m)`$
([Equation 1](#S2.E1 "In 2.3 Memory leveraging: evidence recall and response generation ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")),
which acts as an implicit reranker at no extra LLM call: high-support
items naturally rise to the Top-$`K`$, deprecated items naturally sink,
and the entropy-based reliability $`\rho_{t}`$
([Equation 3](#S2.E3 "In 2.4 Memory evolution: regime-gated belief updating ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents"))
further damps unreliable updates when the score mass is diffuse. This is
what produces the bounded retrieval contamination in
[Table 20](#A3.T20 "In Headline metrics and retrieval contamination. ‣ C.7 Memory dynamics: footprint, contamination, and retention ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
and the bounded effective memory in
[Figure 5](#S3.F5 "In 3.5 Memory scalability ‣ 3 Experiments ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")(b,c),
both of which scale to the 1050-turn DiMoBench-XL run without recourse
to a maintenance loop.

#### Coupling the two axes.

The two axes are complementary: Axis 1 controls *what is committed* so
that the belief store stays faithful to the user’s core traits under
noise, while Axis 2 controls *what surfaces at read time* so that the
dialogue agent sees a coherent, currently-supported view. Existing
baselines optimize one axis at a time (e.g., EverMemOS’s profile updates
target Axis 1; GAM’s deep-research targets Axis 2) but lack a single
mechanism that couples them. PreCoMem couples them through the same
scalar confidence weight $`w_{t}`$, which is the structural reason
behind the consistent improvements observed across the four main
benchmarks.

### C.11 System inspectability, traces, and qualitative analyses

#### Memory store serialization and example snapshot.

To make the contents and interface of the Belief Store concrete, we
describe how a belief item is serialized in practice and show an example
snapshot from a DiMoBench run at turn 210, i.e., right after the
change-point session. Each belief item $`m\in\mathcal{B}_{t}`$ carries
(i) a natural-language proposition $`c(m)`$, (ii) a type label
$`\tau(m)\in\{\texttt{FACT},\texttt{HYPOTHESIS}\}`$, (iii) a confidence
weight $`w_{t}(m)\in[0,1]`$, (iv) a creation timestamp and last-updated
turn, and (v) pointers into the episodic cache $`\mathcal{E}_{t}`$ for
provenance. A minimal serialization is shown in
[Figure 10](#A3.F10 "In Memory store serialization and example snapshot. ‣ C.11 System inspectability, traces, and qualitative analyses ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents").

Figure 10: Compact view of a Belief Store snapshot. Each entry shows the
type, natural-language content, confidence weight, and pointers to the
episodic cache. FACT items are committed, HYPOTHESIS items are
tentative; confidences modulate retrieval priority but are never shown
to the dialogue agent directly.

#### What the dialogue agent actually sees.

Confidence weights $`w_{t}(m)`$ and other metadata are *not* exposed to
the response-generation LLM. They only modulate retrieval ranking
through the confidence-weighted score in
[Equation 1](#S2.E1 "In 2.3 Memory leveraging: evidence recall and response generation ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents"),
so that high-confidence items surface near the top of
$`\mathcal{C}_{t}`$. The dialogue agent receives a plain-text prompt
constructed from the Top-$`K`$ item contents
$`\{c(m)\}_{m\in\mathcal{C}_{t}}`$ together with the relevant
episodic-cache excerpts. This keeps generation-time inputs close to a
standard retrieval-augmented prompt while still letting the memory
system use confidences internally.

#### Inspectability.

Because every belief carries explicit type, confidence, and source
pointers, snapshots like
[Figure 10](#A3.F10 "In Memory store serialization and example snapshot. ‣ C.11 System inspectability, traces, and qualitative analyses ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
can be dumped at any turn for inspection, regression testing, or
qualitative error analysis, without re-executing the model. This also
makes it straightforward to audit which episodic-cache turns support
each currently-held belief, which we use for the end-to-end traces
below.

#### End-to-end trace examples on DiMoBench.

Below we provide two end-to-end traces that expose PreCoMem’s internal
read–write decisions on a DiMoBench example dialogue. For readability,
we list the retrieved belief set $`\mathcal{C}_{t}`$ by the episodic
source turns of its items, without retrieval weights/scores, and we
report only the qualitative regime of effective surprisal
($`x_{t}\!=\!S_{\mathrm{eff}}`$) relative to
$`(\theta_{\mathrm{low}},\theta_{\mathrm{high}})`$.

#### Trace format.

Each trace includes (i) the newly observed dialogue excerpt $`u_{t}`$
(one or a few turns) that triggers a memory write decision, (ii) a
compact view of the retrieved evidence set $`\mathcal{C}_{t}`$, (iii)
the selected evolution operator (MAINTAIN/PROFILE/CORRECT), and (iv) a
subsequent QA query answered using the updated memory state.

#### Trace 1 (change point): early drift signal triggers PROFILE.

Pre-change belief snapshot (high confidence). Mike enjoys crowded
gatherings and rarely skips weekend social events (e.g., D1:5 “The more
the merrier, I love that vibe …”; D1:7 “I wouldn’t miss weekend
gatherings …”).

(Write) New observation $`u_{t}`$ (Session 21).

> D21:6 “Someone invited me out, but I said work’s been busy and didn’t
> go.”
> D21:8 “This weekend I went to a cafe alone, found a book and read for
> the whole afternoon.”
> D21:9 “The environment was pretty quiet, not bad.”

Recall: Top-$`K`$ evidence $`\mathcal{C}_{t}`$.

> D1:5 “The more the merrier, I love that vibe …”
> D1:7 “…I wouldn’t miss weekend gatherings …”
> D2:9 “…I really can’t understand the joy of being a homebody.”
> D3:7 “If I missed a gathering like this, I’d definitely have FOMO,
> afraid of missing out on the fun.”
> D3:10 “Rest can wait, can’t miss the weekend activities! …”
> D9:9 “If one day I don’t want to socialize, the sun must be rising
> from the west …”
> D21:1 “Last weekend’s gathering, the atmosphere got a bit awkward.”

Reliability-adjusted surprisal. The retrieved set consists of strong
*pre-change* evidence (social enthusiasm) and a single emerging
*change-point* cue (D21:1), none of which closely matches the new
observation (skipped invitation; solo activity). The score mass is
therefore spread over several items (higher entropy), making retrieval
reliability $`\rho_{t}`$ moderate. Contradiction signals are also
non-decisive: “work’s been busy” can explain a single skip, so
$`d_{\mathrm{conf}}`$ is non-zero but not saturated. Overall, $`x_{t}`$
falls into the medium regime
($`\theta_{\mathrm{low}}<x_{t}\leq\theta_{\mathrm{high}}`$).

Evolution: PROFILE. PreCoMem writes a low-confidence hypothesis
capturing a *possible* shift, e.g., “Mike may be starting to reduce
large gatherings and explore quiet solo activities,” and links it to
D21:6/D21:8--9 in the episodic cache. Crucially, PreCoMem does *not*
immediately correct the long-standing high-confidence “social
enthusiast” belief, avoiding premature overwrite from a single ambiguous
event.

(Read) QA query. *Q:* “Is Mike still as enthusiastic about gatherings as
before?”
*A:* “Seems to be declining …”
Using the newly written hypothesis plus the concrete episode (D21:6
skipped invitation; D21:8--9 solo cafe), PreCoMem answers with a
*trend-aware* response (declining / becoming evasive) while remaining
cautious about permanence. In later sessions, repeated consistent cues
(e.g., D23:3 “declined most invitations”) reinforce and eventually
promote this hypothesis.

#### Trace 2 (post-change, temporal decoy): reliable contradiction triggers CORRECT.

Precondition. After multiple post-change sessions, PreCoMem has
accumulated consistent evidence favoring solitude (e.g., D28:2 “Without
gatherings I don’t feel bored anymore …”). However, an older pre-change
statement remains in memory as a potential temporal decoy:

> D1:8 “If there’s no gathering on the weekend, I feel like I’d be bored
> to death …”

(Write) New observation $`u_{t}`$ (Session 33).

> D33:9 “Used to say I’d be bored without socializing, now being alone
> is actually more fulfilling.”

Recall: Top-$`K`$ evidence $`\mathcal{C}_{t}`$.

> D1:8 “…bored to death …”
> D22:6 “This weekend I’m planning to stay home, maybe watch some shows
> or something.”
> D23:2 “Used to think quiet places like that were boring, now I find
> them comfortable.”
> D23:3 “…declined most gathering invitations …”
> D25:2 “Used to think being alone was boring, now I actually enjoy
> it.”
> D25:4 “…being alone is recharging, socializing …drains energy.”
> D28:1 “Used to look forward to gatherings on weekends, now I look
> forward to alone time.”
> D28:2 “Without gatherings I don’t feel bored anymore …”
> D33:2 “Reducing socializing …not so exhausted anymore.”

Reliability-adjusted surprisal. Here the recall distribution is more
concentrated because multiple items explicitly mention the disputed
claim (“bored without socializing”) and its reversal, yielding higher
$`\rho_{t}`$. The contradiction signal is strong: D33:9 explicitly
negates the pre-change stance in D1:8, so $`d_{\mathrm{conf}}`$ is high.
Consequently, $`x_{t}`$ falls into the high regime
($`x_{t}>\theta_{\mathrm{high}}`$).

Evolution: CORRECT. PreCoMem applies soft decay
([Equation 10](#S2.E10 "In CORRECT (soft correction). ‣ 2.4 Memory evolution: regime-gated belief updating ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents"))
to the contradicted belief(s) retrieved from $`\mathcal{B}_{t}`$ that
encode the old stance (e.g., “weekends without gatherings are boring”),
while leaving non-contradictory items largely unchanged. Because
confidence weights directly modulate future retrieval
([Equation 1](#S2.E1 "In 2.3 Memory leveraging: evidence recall and response generation ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")),
this correction reduces the chance of “old-memory pull” when later
queries contain temporal-decoy phrasing.

(Read) QA query. *Q:* “Would Mike still say ‘I’d be bored without
socializing’?”
*A:* “No, being alone is actually more fulfilling now.”
PreCoMem retrieves the recent, high-support solitude evidence (D33:9,
D28:2) and answers accordingly, while older evidence like D1:8 is
downweighted by correction and recency, making the response robust to
the temporal decoy.

![Refer to caption](2610.29467v1/case_study.png)

Figure 11: Qualitative analysis of evidence consolidation. Left: In
skill calibration, PreCoMem weighs credentials against contradictory
behavioral cues to avoid over-estimation. Right: In project reasoning,
it distinguishes high-confidence competencies from tentative learning
(“loading”), preventing premature recommendations.

#### Qualitative case studies beyond accuracy.

[Figure 11](#A3.F11 "In Trace 2 (post-change, temporal decoy): reliable contradiction triggers CORRECT. ‣ C.11 System inspectability, traces, and qualitative analyses ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
shows two cases in which the answer depends on how evidence was
consolidated; a third, constructed case walks through a new topic
entering the store.

#### Case 1: Skill level calibration.

[Figure 11](#A3.F11 "In Trace 2 (post-change, temporal decoy): reliable contradiction triggers CORRECT. ‣ C.11 System inspectability, traces, and qualitative analyses ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")
(left) illustrates this case. The user initially reports a credential
(CET-6), which can tempt a retrieval-only system to over-trust a single
strong signal and recommend overly advanced practice. PreCoMem instead
consolidates evidence across sessions: repeated behavioral cues (e.g.,
failing to articulate at an English corner) and professional feedback
(teacher assessment) lower confidence in an over-optimistic
self-evaluation, while preserving the belief that the foundation is
adequate. At query time, Memory Leveraging recalls these calibrated
beliefs (rather than a single credential trace) to condition response
generation, leading to a plan that targets fluency and practice rather
than premature advanced content.

#### Case 2: Project experience reasoning.

Long-horizon career discussions often mix stable skills (proven
experience) with tentative claims (currently learning) and difficulty
signals (struggles with complex systems). PreCoMem uses its
confidence-weighted belief store to separate what is established (e.g.,
Python/Flask) from what is still emerging (e.g., React “loading”) and
what should be deferred (e.g., microservices architecture). This
produces right-sized recommendations: it proposes a near-term path
aligned with demonstrated capability, while explicitly marking a
challenge zone for later progression.

#### Case 3: A new topic entering a populated store.

Suppose the store holds the user’s work and food profile but nothing
about music, and the user says for the first time: “I signed up for
guitar lessons and practiced for an hour last night.” No belief matches
and the Top-$`K`$ items are only loosely related, so absolute scores are
low but one still dominates, giving a moderate $`\rho_{t}`$ and, by the
mechanism described in
[Section C.8](#A3.SS8.SSS0.Px4 "Cold start and genuinely new topics. ‣ C.8 Retrieval dependence and reliability calibration ‣ Appendix C Additional experimental details ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents"),
PROFILE. The Profile-Agent writes the hypothesis “the user may have
taken up guitar” with $`w_{0}<\tau_{\mathrm{promote}}`$, linked by
provenance to that turn and retrieval-visible at once, so the agent can
already answer cautiously. If guitar recurs, MAINTAIN reinforces the
hypothesis until it crosses $`\tau_{\mathrm{promote}}`$ and becomes a
FACT; otherwise it remains a low-confidence hypothesis that never
outranks committed facts. A new topic therefore enters provisionally, is
usable immediately, and is committed only after repeated support.

#### Connection to the consolidation loop.

Across these cases, PreCoMem’s leveraging–evolution loop links response
quality to memory consolidation. Evidence recalled for the current
answer is also used to update confidence through
MAINTAIN/PROFILE/CORRECT
([Section 2.4](#S2.SS4 "2.4 Memory evolution: regime-gated belief updating ‣ 2 PreCoMem ‣ PreCoMem: Predictive Cognitive Memory for Self-Evolving Long-Term Dialogue Agents")),
so future retrieval and recommendations reflect accumulated support,
implicit verification, and soft correction under contradiction.

## Appendix D Prompts

All prompts used in this paper are released verbatim with the code at
[https://github.com/ZhongWishing/PreCoMem](https://github.com/ZhongWishing/PreCoMem).
The three sub-agent prompts (Profile-Agent, Correct-Agent,
Maintain-Agent) and the response-generation prompt are in
precomem/llm/prompts.py; the QA and LLM-as-a-judge prompts for LoCoMo,
LongMemEval-S, PersonaMem-v2, and DiMoBench are in
precomem/evaluation/prompts.py; and the DiMoBench generation and
verification prompts are in precomem/dimobench/prompts.py.
````
